Since the logic of refutation is twofold—one part establishing what is proper, the other overturning the opposing view—we shall first set forth our pious Christian faith by means of natural reasonings, with demonstration as far as possible, and then we shall attempt to refute those things that seem to support his impious doctrine: partly by natural means from common notions about God, and partly by Scripture from which the very author of such a book appears to wish to admit testimony. I shall begin from this point.
The all-wise, good, infinitely powerful, and all-holy God, while bringing about the constitution and existence of the world from things that did not exist, either created and produced it as being powerful in power, or as not being powerful in power. If not in power, then those accurate and common notions about God which are customary and prevalent among men are false; for how could the same Being have bound and fitted these things together and brought them into existence? Or how could He be the producer and creator, and how could the very name of creation, or of such an operation, be firmly attributed to Him, if He were circumscribed by powerlessness and the infirmity of nature, and were thought of and believed in as an impotent God? For the name of God is, as I think, none other than that which is significatory and indicative of creative activity; since to be...
defined and characterized by a limited power in its being. But if this is so, then it is not eternal; it follows, therefore, that God is not eternal, which is absurd. But if, on the other hand, God produces all generated things by multiple limited powers, the Divine is in no way infinite in power; and it will coincide with the same former hypothesis; and from either hypothesis, it follows that the Divine is in no way infinite in power. Since, therefore, the eternal is by nature infinite power, or as it were an infinite space, as it happens to seem to the sciences and to the truth, it is clear that what is eternal is also infinite in power. B It is syllogized, therefore, as follows: That which is eternal is infinite in power, and what does not have infinite power is therefore also not eternal. If, therefore, in neither hypothesis God has infinite power, in no way is He eternal. But if He is not eternal, He is necessarily subject to time: for of existing things, some are eternal, others are bounded by time, and are subject to the creator of time. But if this is so, He has a beginning and an end, and there will be some other cause of His existence, and regarding that, we will encounter the same difficulties; and this will go on to infinity, which is absurd. It remains, therefore, of the remaining two, that the other is true, which asserts one and infinite power; but that it is improper to speak of God as having multiple and infinite powers
against the many, and infinite in all respects, which is truer and more fitting to concepts of God
. Because the Word, from common notions and through scientific proofs, has shown that it is by one alone, and simple, and in all respects infinite power, that God possesses the power to do what He does, and by which He gave substance to the world and brought it forth, and is said to have the power to be upon it. So therefore, this power is what is in God—not that He is sometimes powerless and weak to create and bring forth; for if He were ever potentially powerless, it would be difficult to explain. If this is so, and willing is not concurrent, how then can one separate the generated nature, since the power to act is not always present with Him? But the common notion holds firmly to this, that God can do all things, and when He wills, at the time He wills. But if this is so, common notion does not always stick to it. It is reckless, therefore, to hold this opinion, whatever it may be.
7. And otherwise: If God, as we have said, at some time receives this power, it is clear that this is an acquired power, if God is, indeed, eternal; and that which is received adventitiously is received later than His being, and is received by progress and as if He were lacking. It follows, therefore, that God will be incomplete and perfected by progress; which is the same as saying and thinking blasphemy regarding God. And otherwise: If the power of God is not always present, but is received later; and if this is so, whether it is from Himself
or from another. But if it is from Himself, it is as though He possessed this power—or as though He did not possess it, and then took it. For it is impossible for one who does not possess something to be able to do anything; and for one who possesses it, it is impossible for that which he possesses to be—first, because the barrier is eternal, and it is not possible not to possess it; second, it will be found that it is a power over and above that which is already defined as power, which has been proven to be false. If, then, He did not receive it from Himself, it remains that He received it from another. But if from another, whether from an equal, or from one lesser, or from one greater. But that it cannot be from an equal—for by this it is clear: the one being perfected is perfected by the greater; the one that is lesser is inferior; and the one that is equal to the similar is greater than God is not possible. For if there were such, there would be God, and then another God, and this to infinity. Therefore, God did not receive His power from another.
8. Since, therefore, God receives His power neither from Himself, nor—and much less—from another, it is clear that He has it congenitally. And He is always Himself; therefore, also His wisdom, by which He is powerful and by which He acts, always is. That which is eternal is also perpetual and timeless. Now, that which always is, is eternal and perpetual and timeless, and unbegotten, and not made. It is gathered, therefore, that the power of God, by which He is powerful, and by which He acts, and without which He does nothing, is unbegotten and uncreated. But if that which is produced is not from the unproduced, in so far as it is not produced, and the uncreated is not from the created, then in nature there is no difference between them; and if they do not differ, they are the same; and if they are the same, they are likewise consubstantial, and whatever is of this kind. But God is unbegotten and uncreated; therefore, His power is likewise. Moreover, since they hold common notions correctly that nature and that which is uncreated are the same, it is gathered, therefore, that the power of God, by which He is called powerful, and by which He acts, and without which He does nothing, is consubstantial and of the same nature as God. As these things are so, the power of God itself also arises from these things in a way that we do not foolishly shout, as the Agarenes do against us, but as our argument will demonstrate; since the Spirit Himself, who is in God as of the same nature and consubstantial with God of His power, and as the Word of God and the Wisdom of God is called, it would clearly declare—but that the power of God by which He is called powerful, and by which He acts, and without which He does nothing, and the Word of God is called an image [(4)], is not subsequent in nature, as you say, but of the same nature and consubstantial with God as unbegotten and uncreated, and this here is clear.
9. God is extremely simple and superior to any composite notion, as the common [index: entries preserved]
notions dictate to us. For everything that is incidental to that with which it is involved, is composite and is reckoned as such. But if this is so, and it is dissoluble, or exists by act or by potentiality, that is, it is reasoned or subsists somehow; therefore it is to be syllogized thus: God is most simple; whatever is incidental to that with which it is involved is not simple and is reckoned as such; it is concluded therefore that God is incapable of accidents. But He has power, as has been demonstrated; therefore this also is not an accident; just as this is to be concluded: God is incapable of accidents; God is possessed of power: it is concluded therefore that power is not a piece of an accident, but rather possesses self-subsistence and is per se, and requires nothing else; it is also and always, as this also has been demonstrated. That which is always, possesses neither temporal beginning nor end. Everything that is self-subsistent, having neither temporal beginning nor end, is uncreated and unbegotten. It is found therefore that the power of God is self-subsistent, and is both Word and Wisdom alongside the divine Scriptures plainly stated, is uncreated and unbegotten; and the uncreated and unbegotten, and the common notions are not accustomed to say it is of another, but of God: the power therefore of God, which is also called His Wisdom and Word and Son by us, is God, and is consistent as like to God and the Father, and is believed and reckoned by those who properly believe as Christians. For that one, the power of God, is not said merely, but it is by which He acts, and without which God does nothing, the Word and consubstantial with Him and of the same nature as God and the Father, with precise demonstration, has made clear.
10. But that the same is the Son of God and Word and Wisdom, and is deservedly so confessed and believed by us Christians, is made manifest thus. God, if He is the true God and is said and believed by those of correct thought to be glorified and preached, that is, from those that are of Him, that is, from His substance, is known, in which He is glorified and preached and believed to be, and from those that are about Him, that is, His creatures. But for those that are about Him, that is, to be known from His substance, is impossible and unthinkable by angelic powers, because by human nature, and common notions teach us this. For God, according to the account of substance, is unnameable and ineffable and anonymous, and the divine Scriptures and common notions hand this down; of which there is clear proof, if one wishes, as he sees, that truly none of His names are indicative of the substance itself; for indeed each of the names taken for the guidance of the divine nature has a particular meaning embraced in it; and you would not find any word without some meaning among the most divine of names. If then it is impossible to know Him from the substance, as we have said, it is evident that from those things that are about Him, just as any upright person indicates; for from greatness and beauty [index: entries preserved]
of creatures, the Creator is known. And if someone should not grant even this—namely, that He is known from the things about Him, that is, from what is in us—our being entirely ignorant of God would necessarily follow, which is absurd. If, then, God is to be known from what is in us, it is evident that He is known from the things which are more esteemed and more honorable among us—this we described and affirmed—and we do not exclude from Him things which are in us as accidents, for we designate them as attributes of God. Therefore, whatever is best in us, we affirm of Him, or rather we negate all things from Him. And we do not think that affirmations conflict with negations, because both are not taken from the same source. But let our speech be clearer, so that I may speak.
11. In us, indeed, there are many and diverse things, and some are taken in a contrary sense: some are worthy and better, others have a worse condition. It is clear, therefore, that if we are led by the hand toward the knowledge of God from what is in us, we describe and affirm God according to our better qualities. For there is light and darkness in us, life and death, mortality and immortality, justice and iniquity; and the former are placed in better categories, such as life and immortality, justice, charity; the latter, however, in worse ones, such as death, mortality, iniquity, hatred. It is explored that life and immortality, justice and charity, and what is consistent with these, are to be attributed to God by us, and He is described and defined by them to us; nay, to speak more properly, we deny that these are in Him as they are in us. For God is above light, above immortality, above justice, and whatever is of this sort. Likewise, in us there is sight and blindness; namely, if we omit blindness, we say God is impassible in sight; that is, apart from those things which occur to us by visual means; for in all things God is impassible. For even if we possess these scriptures, we do not investigate God by these traces. And so, because there is fertility and barrenness in us, generation and sterility, fruitfulness and unfruitfulness; the better, indeed, are generation and fertility, germination and such like: the worse, however, are sterility, unfruitfulness, and the privation of growth; but we describe and define God through the better qualities, apart from those things that occur to us as accidents, as we say; that is, indubitably, apart from those things that have generation, fertility, and fruit and such like. Yet if someone in these matters should contend, I will also contend, that death is better than life, darkness than light; which is ridiculous. Therefore it is evident that God is fertile, and the progenitor of the Word.
C known through the analogy of the creatures; for if anyone should not grant that He is to be known from things that pertain to Him—that is, from things within ourselves—and yet we have never known Him according to the account of His essence, it would be necessary that we remain in total ignorance of God, which is absurd. If, therefore, God is known from things within us, it is evident that we describe Him from those things in us that are more worthy and honorable, without the accidents that happen to us; for we remove these from what is said of God. For this reason, we affirm of Him all things that are better in us, and yet we deny all of them of Him more fundamentally. And we do not consider the affirmations to be in conflict with the negations, for they were not taken from the same things. In order that my discourse may be clear to us, I shall speak thus: 11. In us, there are many and various things, some taken as if in opposition, with some being more worthy and better, and others having the part of the worse. It is evident, then, that when we are led by the hand from things within us toward the guidance of God, we describe and affirm Him from that which is better and more honorable in us. If, therefore, there is in us light and darkness, life and death, mortality and immortality, justice and injustice, and some are placed in the category of the better—such as life and immortality, justice and love—while others are in that of the worse—such as death, mortality, injustice, and hatred—it is clear that we attribute life and immortality, justice and love, and whatever is consonant with these, to God, and through these we describe and affirm Him; and, to speak more precisely, we deny these of Him; for God is beyond light, beyond immortality, beyond justice, and whatever is similar to these. Likewise, there is in us sight and blindness. Leaving aside the blindness, we shall say that God is sighted impassibly, that is, without the passions that occur in us along with sight; for in all things God is impassible. Even if we had never received the Scripture, we would trace God by such footprints and methods. Since, therefore, there is in us both fertility and barrenness, generation and sterility, fruitfulness and unfruitfulness, sprouting and the privation of this, and the better are, for example, generation and fertility, sprouting and all that is similar to these, while the worse are, for example, barrenness, sterility, unfruitfulness, and the privation of sprouting; and since we describe and affirm God through the better things, without the accidents that occur in us, as we have said, it is evident that we shall say He possesses fertility, generation, a sprout, fruit, and things similar to these. For if anyone were to contend in these matters, I too would contend that death is better than life, and darkness than light, which is ridiculous. Therefore, it is manifest that God is fertile and the producer of the Word; for it is beyond all controversy that the generative power is more honorable than sterility and [original-script column: see facsimile]
barrenness; if this be so, it is clear that God possesses a Son. For it is possible to argue thus: 12. We aptly describe God as generative and fertile, blossoming forth from the superior qualities that exist within us; and everything that exists in such a manner has an orientation toward a son, or a sprout, or a fruit, or offspring. It is gathered, therefore, that God, being appropriately described from the things that are in us, also has a Son (and this is rightly believed and glorified among us), namely the Word who was generated from Him enhypostatically, and impassibly, and without flux. But if this is so, it is manifest even to those able to see only dimly that the enhypostatic power of God and His Wisdom—by which He is powerful, and through which He acts, and without which He does nothing—which also our discourse has demonstrated to be consubstantial with God the Father and similar in all respects, leaving aside the generation, is indeed the Son of God, and Wisdom, and Word, and is deservedly confessed and believed by us Christians. If this is true, as it has been demonstrated to be true, then the barbarian and irrational Mohammed in the book forged by him mocks us in vain as "associators" and "partners," as though we were bringing in from the outside another God, the Son of God the Father, in addition to the one God by nature and in truth. If, therefore, our discourse, from common notions and logical methods, has clearly shown the power of God—by which He is powerful, and through which He acts, and without which He does nothing—which also is and is called the Wisdom and Word of God the Father, to be enhypostatic and consubstantial and in all respects similar to God the Father, and to be the same as the Son, as is rightly believed among us, it has most clearly banished and cast away, and exposed as liars, you who—while you confess the things after the unity of nature and the things in the property, that is, the hypostasis—do not accept the Father and the Son as consubstantial and identical in all things, except for the unbegottenness and the generation. Therefore, the discourse of our construction appeared to us perfect, and lacking nothing for those who desire—insofar as it is possible from common notions and physical arguments—to receive demonstrations for things exceeding nature: namely, that God the Father has a Son similar to Himself in all things, whom He has begotten impassibly and intemporally and without flux; who also is the Word and Wisdom and enhypostatic Power of God, and is rightly believed by us.
13. But since the Spirit who proceeds from God the Father, and who together with the Son vivifies the world and recreates it and transforms it toward a better life, is of the same nature and consubstantial with the Father and the Son and of equal honor, being one hypostasis of the one true Godhead (as it is also rightly believed and proclaimed among us Christians), come, I will also institute a discourse concerning Him, and I will present the demonstrations rather from common notions and from scriptural sayings, so that the doctrine of faith concerning the holy Trinity according to the properties, that is, the hypostases and the one Godhead and lordship and authority and monarchy may be kept intact and be rejected by no one of the other faith. For it is evident to us from the use of common notions; but besides this, the inspiration of the prophets is supplied by the divine Spirit, and it clearly preserves and cherishes, vivifies, and bestows divine instincts and charisms and different methods of sanctification upon those who are worthy, just as your own scripture also speaks in harmony with these things; for it is explored that God is far prior, and is holy, and by divine custom guards the world and leads it to a condition of better morals. Now if by chance some madman would not concede this, and would say that God is lacking in all holiness. But how does He offer the bounties of divine grace to all who desire to draw from them without any diminution of His own? For if He Himself lacks holiness, surely He does not sanctify: for no one gives what he does not have to anyone; for he who labors under poverty himself, would doubt it to others. If God, therefore, is not a participant in holiness, without doubt the whole of creation will be far more deserted of grace and divine gift. And yet creation is not at all established without the protection of grace and holiness; for otherwise how would it subsist? Therefore it is manifest that He who bestows these things, that is, God, is the more substantial in the fullness of holiness, and by that holiness governs the world. If, therefore, God in His holiness sanctifies and directs the world, and fills the existing world with divine inspirations and doctrines and charisms, it is manifest that He Himself is not without that holiness. Furthermore, whatever has holiness has it either as adopted or as natural. Now if it has it as adopted, it can be known by a syllogism to be absurd. For if God has adopted holiness and inspiration, yet since everything is possessed in this way, it is perfected by progressing: it is concluded, therefore, that God is perfected by progressing. Which cannot be said of God without blasphemy.
14. Item, otherwise. If God has his holiness and inspiration in an adopted way, it is manifest he received it from the outside: but everyone who has so received, receives from another; for no existing thing gives itself to itself and is given, is sanctified and is an illuminator; just as the same man does not teach and is taught. If therefore God receives holiness from another, he either receives it from a lesser, or from an equal, or from a greater. But since he receives from none of these, it follows that: for he who receives holiness from another, is sanctified by the one who gives; but he who is sanctified by receiving, receives from a greater and is sanctified. Furthermore, one of these is inferior, the other equal; but common sense persuades us that no one is better than God. Therefore God did not receive his holiness from another. And if not...
from another, it is clear that he did not receive it from outside, nor by adoption; for everything that is adopted is likewise so.
15. Furthermore: If God has his holiness by adoption, it is clear that it has been added to him. Whatever is added is limited, and it is evident that it exists in a created nature. It follows, therefore, that God is a created being, and possesses a created nature. If this is so, then he was either created by himself or by another. But if he were created by himself, he would be at once the producer and the product, the creator and the created, the one making and the one being made; but nothing among things that exist is of such a nature. So God is not created by himself. B Indeed, he is not created by another; for if anyone were to concede this, there would be a God of God, and the same ambiguity would follow regarding him; and so on to infinity. Therefore God does not have his holiness and the inspiration of his gifts by adoption. If, then, the Logos, through many arguments, has shown it impossible for God to possess his holiness and inspiration by adoption, it is evident that he possesses it by nature. If, then, he possesses it by nature, whether necessarily or mostly, it is clear—for that which happens only mostly is a defect of nature; for nature sometimes fails in those things which mostly happen, and even in necessary things. But failure does not befall the divine nature; since all failure is not without passion. God is entirely above every passion. Therefore, God possesses his own holiness not mostly, but necessarily. If it is necessary, and he is always, since such things are necessary, then the holiness and the inspiration of God is always in him, that is to say, it is enhypostatic, and has existence in itself, and is not heterohypostatic nor an accident; but since it is not an accident that exists in another, it is clear how it subsists.
16. God is most simple, and is above every mental composition or composite concept, as common notions hold; whatever is an accident in that to which it is added is composite and is so reckoned. Therefore, to reason thus: God is most simple; but whatever is an accident in that to which it is added is not simple and is so reckoned; it follows, therefore, that God, being without accident, possesses in himself the holiness and inspiration, which is also called the Spirit of God, to which your scripture also bears witness—that is, the book of Muhammad. Since it is not an accident, it is enhypostatic and has existence in itself; and it is always—for everything that is enhypostatic, and has existence in itself, and is, has neither temporal beginning nor end. What is in such a state is by all means uncreated and unbegotten. It follows, therefore, that the holiness and inspiration of God, which is also called the Spirit by divine Scripture, is unbegotten and uncreated; and being unbegotten with the unbegotten,
and uncreated with the uncreated, it is the same in nature; and since it is the same, it is homogeneous and consubstantial. It is evident, therefore, that the holiness of God is enhypostatic, and is clearly called Spirit by the divine Scriptures, and is truly God, consubstantial with God and the Father and His Son.
17. If, therefore, God the Father, as is agreeable to all common conceptions, is enhypostatic and has His existence always and in Himself: it has been demonstrated that the power of God also, by which He is powerful and by which He acts, and without which He does nothing, and which is called the Word and Wisdom of God, always exists and is enhypostatic. Similarly, holiness, which is called the Spirit of God, is also enhypostatic and perpetual, and has subsistence in itself. But whatever is perpetual, together with that which is perpetual and enhypostatic, and with the enhypostatic, is found to be three homogeneous and consubstantial hypostases. And herein for us are God the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit: the one, the Begetter and the Progenerator, if I may so speak, impassibly, timelessly, and incorporeally; the other, the generation; the third, the procession. For if the Father is the true God, and the Son and the Holy Spirit are of the same and one Godhead, indivisible as they are undivided; thus, for us, they are contemplated—yet they are not three gods. And that this is true, as I promised above, I shall endeavor to show. All number is indicative of the quantity of the subjects, but not of the nature of the things; and it is so not insofar as they are the same, but insofar as they are diverse. Likewise, also, enumeration, that is, a gathering into one number, is in a way an otherness, or is a synthesis; and this is clear if one takes gold and breaks it down into five characters: he will not say five golds as to the nature, but five golden characters, so as to signify the quantity of the things underlying. And these are the same, yet different; and in like manner, when they are enumerated, that is, gathered into one number of five, they introduce the otherness of the synthesis. For number is of diverse things, and is inclusive and comprehensive insofar as they are diverse.
18. Having set these things forth for us, mark well what is proved. Since the divine and blessed nature is entirely, as our discourse shows, in the Father and in the Son and in the Holy Spirit; and there is not in this anything prior or posterior, nor is it signified by a predicate, but it is rather strictly coeternal and in every way similar: for it is thought of as the whole, either as parts, or in the participants themselves; and this is again viewed as one whole, as a whole indivisible, or even in the others. I say "whole," not that which is composed and agglutinated from parts; far be it; but that which is viewed as a nature in its own hypostases.
Since that which is not originated is the same in nature as the underived, and that which is uncreated is the same in nature as the uncreated, if they are the same, they are also of the same nature and consubstantial; hence it is manifest that the enhypostatic holiness of God, which is also clearly called "Spirit" by the divine Scriptures, is true God, consubstantial with God the Father and his Son. B 17. If, therefore, God the Father, as follows from all common notions, is enhypostatic and possesses his being always and in himself; and it has been demonstrated that the power of God—by which he is powerful, and through which he acts, and without which he acts in nothing, which also is and is called the Word and Wisdom of God—is also both eternal and enhypostatic; likewise also the holiness, which is also called the Spirit of God, is enhypostatic and possesses its being always and in itself; then one would find the eternal and eternal and eternal, and the enhypostatic with the enhypostatic and the enhypostatic to be of one nature and consubstantial. And this to us is God the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit; the one being the Generator and Producer, I say, impassibly, timelessly, and incorporeally; of the others, the one is the generation, the other the procession. For even if the Father is true God, and the Son and the Holy Spirit likewise, as of one and the same divinity, viewed indivisibly as in things that are divided, so to speak, nonetheless they are not three gods. And that this is true, as I promised above, I shall attempt to show. Every number is indicative of the quantity of the subjects, not of the nature of the things; and this not insofar as they are the same, but insofar as they are different. Similarly, all enumeration—that is, the gathering into one number—of diversities, insofar as it is other, is a composition and a gathering; and this is clear if someone were to take gold and break it into five coin-stamps, he would not say "five golds" so as to designate the nature, but "five gold stamps," so as to indicate the quantity of the subjects; and these not insofar as they are the same, but insofar as they are different. Likewise, these things too, when enumerated—that is, when the five are gathered into one number—introduce a composition of the things that are different in some way. For indeed, the number of different things, insofar as they are different, would be encompassing and inclusive. C It is being established [that] the divine and blessed nature, as a whole, insofar as it is whole, is viewed indivisibly in the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, as the discourse has shown; and not more in one and less in another, but the predication is made equally and in like manner; and the predication signifies not one thing prior and another posterior, but something coeternal and in all respects like. For the whole is conceived either before the parts, or in those that partake of it; and of these, the whole is viewed as a whole indivisibly either in the one or in the rest. I call "whole," however, not that which is composed and compounded from parts—far be it—but that which, being as a nature, is viewed in its proper hypostases. From what has been said, I say, it is evident that the Father...
and the Son and the Holy Spirit, according to one divinity, power, and lordship, are identified and are one, since they possess the same and single divinity equally. If, therefore, God the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, in respect to the one divinity and power, possess it equally, they are one and are believed to be so. It is acknowledged that every number is indicative of the quantity of subjects, and not of the nature of things, and of these things not as they are identical, but as they are different. Furthermore, every enumeration—that is, the reduction of diversities under one number, as they are different—is a composition. It is therefore evident that when we speak of the Father individually, and likewise the Son and the Holy Spirit, we do not introduce three gods by numbering the nature; for then the number and the enumeration would belong to the nature and not to the quantity of the subject things, and [to the nature] as they are identical, not as they are different, which is not admitted. Therefore, when we confess the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, we do not declare that they are one as Father, Son, or Holy Spirit (for these are different), but as there is one divinity and power, and one God is considered, even if three are believed in their hypostases.
B 21. It is possible in another way to demonstrate that we do not introduce three gods in respect to the one divinity, even if we confess three hypostases. If, therefore, the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, as we have accurately demonstrated, possess the one, uncreated, and ingenerate divinity co-eternally, and one nature is predicated of the three, and not one [nature] for one and another [nature] for another, it is evident that saying "three hypostases" does not also introduce three gods. For if anyone charges this upon us, he says nothing else but that the same being is both one and many, that the contradiction is true, and that the lesser are predicated of the greater and are made equal to them; which corresponds neither with logical rules nor with the truth. And so that what is said may be clear to us, I will take the human nature as an example, and through the things predicated of it, I will make the statement evident to you. Let the human nature be predicated as in a subject of three hypostases only, for instance, say, Peter, Paul, and Thomas. This nature, therefore, as a whole, is predicated indivisibly of Peter, Paul, and Thomas; for each of these is a rational, mortal animal, receptive of mind and knowledge. It is evident that these participants, in that they participate in the same and single nature, are one and the same; but in that they are different in their hypostases, they possess the number three. If, therefore, anyone uses the word "man," which is indicative of nature, which is one and the same and is predicated indivisibly of the three hypostases, and then, as if they were three enumerated things, he should necessarily enumerate this word "man" with the persons, calling them "three men," he says nothing else but that three natures are viewed in the three hypostases; for the word "man" is indicative of nature. But if this is so, it will surely be found that the same thing, according to the same respect—that is, the human nature—is both one and the contrary; and that which is a contradiction will turn out to be true, which is absurd.
And not only this, but it results that the lesser things, that is, the persons, are predicated of the greater, that is, the natures, and are equalized to them; and this is clear in the following way:
A 
κ'. Human nature, when predicated of the three persons, is neither predicated convertibly of them, nor does it convert, because it is in excess of each person. For if something is Peter, it is also man; but if it is man, it is not necessarily Peter. For among predicables, those that are equal convert with one another, such as "man" and "capable of laughter"; for if something is man, it is also capable of laughter, and if it is capable of laughter, it is also man. But those that are in excess do not convert with one another, because of the excess. For instance, if something is man, it is also animal; but if it is animal, it is not necessarily man. If, therefore, human nature is in excess of each of the three persons individually, but the number "three" is predicated of the three persons—not absolutely, but according to the specific quantity of the underlying subjects—and you predicate the same number, which we call "numerable" (as it is numbered rather than acting as a number), of the term "man" as equal to the number of persons, then each person will be found to be equal to the nature, and predicated convertibly. For "three" and what is numbered as "three" are not in excess, but are equal. For even if there are two things, and one is predicated of another—for instance "capable of laughter" of "man"—if it is shown that the other is equal, it happens of necessity that the predication converts, and "man" is predicated of "capable of laughter." Wherefore it is evident that if anyone numbers the name of their nature along with the persons, he compels the lesser to be predicated of the greater and to be equalized; which is discordant with logical methods and with the truth, as we have said.
κα'. The same holds true regarding the divine nature, that we may be guided by this example to grasp the truth and ward off the falsehood. For when we confess three persons in the divine nature, there is no necessity to introduce three gods, on account of the absurdities already shown. Thus it has been made manifest to us that, by confessing three persons according to their properties in the one divine nature, we do not introduce three gods, but one God, because there is one deity, and those things which are of Him have their reference to One. For the names "Father" and "Son" do not signify a nature, but a relation, and how the Father stands in relation to the Son, or the Son to the Father. For just as with us these appellations signify a genuine generation, so there they signify the consubstantiality of the begotten with the begetter. Likewise, the "Spirit" also does not signify a nature, any more than the others do; for the difference in their relation to one another has created a different appellation for them. Therefore, when we look toward the deity, the first cause, and the monarchy, we see one thing; but when…
we look toward those things in which the Godhead consists—those which shine forth from the first cause intemporally and with an equality of honor—there are three to be worshiped; and these are the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, in whom we believe and have been baptized, and whom we serve with latreutic worship.
22. If the descendants of Hagar, in their shamelessness, should proceed against the things spoken, saying: "Even if the name of 'nature' is not properly enumerated, yet it is said to be used abusively according to common parlance, just as we say 'three men' and 'three horses'; therefore, according to you, let the Trinity which you worship be called by an abusive term 'three gods,' just as there are 'three men,' or any other thing that is enumerated"—we respond to them: that the name of 'nature' is then enumerated with its proper persons abusively, when the persons do not possess a circumincession 6 with one another, but stand apart from each other by a great distance, and in addition possess a different will, energy, and intent, and possess a very great difference, but a minimal communion. But for those whose judgment is one, and whose intent is inseparable, and who possess identity of nature, one authority, will, and energy, and everything in common—except for the one difference of the hypostatic properties alone—and for whom the inseparable separation from one another and the circumincession into one another exists, the name of their nature is in no way found to be numerable, neither properly nor abusively. For this reason, even if we confess the Father to be God, and likewise the Son and the Holy Spirit, we do not introduce three gods; nor are we required to enumerate them, neither properly nor abusively, since circumincession is present, and inseparability, and indivisibility, and one judgment, and intent, and energy, and the other things we have mentioned.
But if they should again object, saying that the name 'God' is not indicative of the divine nature, but of an operation 7, and that the names of operations are enumerated—for instance, we say "three orators" and "three philosophers" or "three builders"—we also reply to these points: that such names of operations are enumerated in those cases where a change of judgment slips in, and a diversity of intent is present, and a difficulty calls for a need, and the energy is not one, but different and disjointed. But for those in whom one natural, undivided energy is present, the names of those operations are in no way enumerated. Since, therefore, the supersubstantial Trinity—that is, the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, which is piously and rightly believed and worshiped by Christians—possesses one indivisible energy, it is therefore evident that even if the name 'God' is indicative of an operation, it will have no place for being enumerated in this regard. Whence our faith is exalted above every calumny and accusation, just as has been demonstrated.
23. It is also possible for us, by way of a scientific and inescapable division, to express the things concerning the consubstantial and holy Trinity, and to show the proofs here as well. If God is true God and is believed to be such, he is either one by nature and by hypostasis, or the other way around: one by hypostasis, but many by nature, or one by nature and many by hypostasis—or he is many by nature and many by hypostasis. But if he is many by nature and many by hypostasis, this is clearly an absurdity; for if it is so, it would be polyarchic and seditious; and that which is seditious is anarchic and disordered, and that which is disordered is the beginning of dissolution; and if this is so, it is also corruptible and perishable. But if it happens to be one by hypostasis, but many by nature, this also is an absurdity; for whatever is of such a kind is composite. For everything that is constituted of diverse natures is placed among composite things. Now, the composite is dissoluble; and corruption follows upon dissolution; and annihilation follows upon corruption. Therefore it results that God would be corruptible and non-existent; and this is more absurd than anything. Consequently, since he is proven to be many by nature and by hypostasis, and likewise one by hypostasis but many by nature, it remains that he is one of the two, and that the latter is true. If, therefore, he is one by nature but many by hypostasis, the Word shows that this is the true and firm view, and it is evident that the rest are refuted. And that he is one by nature, but many by hypostases, is clear from this: nature, or the common element, is one thing, and the individual property is another. For nature is something common, or a property; if it is a property, it is individual; and if it is individual, it is a certain atom: and if it exists as such, or if it is an atom that has been destroyed, it is necessary that nature also be destroyed at the same time. For if of those which are identical, one or the other be destroyed, it is necessary that the other also be destroyed. If, therefore, with the death of Moses, human nature had been destroyed, it follows that human nature would have perished. But with the death of Moses, nature was not diminished. Therefore it is not singular nature. Because if it is singular, it is not nature, since it is a property.
24. And otherwise. If nature is a property, it must be predicated of one thing only; but it is predicated of many, like 'rational mortal animal,' of Peter, of Paul, of Thomas, and of each of the humans: therefore it is not a property of a thing. If it is not a property, then it is common. Likewise otherwise. If, therefore, nature is a property and person is a property of the thing of which it is pronounced, it will certainly be found to be a property of the thing, which it cannot actually be at all. Therefore, it is no property; and what is in no way a property is common. Furthermore, if, therefore, nature is common and universal, and is predicated of many; and nature is thus constituted; it follows that nature is something common and universal. Therefore, since the supersubstantial and uncreated divine nature is the divine nature, it is clear that it also is something common and universal; for it has been shown that nature is something common and universal. And the divine and blessed nature is indeed common and universal.
And we may set forth the demonstrations here as well. If God is truly God and is believed as such, He is either one in nature and in person, or one in nature but many in persons; or, conversely, one in person but many in nature; or, finally, many in both nature and person. But if He were many in both nature and person, the absurdity is apparent; for to be so is polyarchy. Polyarchy, moreover, is seditious, and what is seditious is anarchic and disordered; and what is disordered is the beginning of dissolution; if this is the case, it is also corruptible and perishable. If, on the other hand, He were one in person but many in nature, this is also absurd; for that which is of such a kind is necessarily composite. For all that is constituted as one thing from diverse natures is placed in the category of composite things. Now, a composite thing is dissoluble; dissolution is followed by corruption; and corruption is followed by non-existence. It is concluded, therefore, that God is corruptible and lapses into non-existence, which is the height of absurdity. If, then, the position that He is many in nature and person is proven false, and likewise the position that He is one in person but many in nature, it remains that one of the two [remaining] hypotheses must be true. If, therefore, the reasoning shows that to be one in nature and many in persons is true and certain, it is evident that the rest are refuted, and that this is true: that He is one in nature and many in persons becomes clear from the following: Nature is either a common thing or a peculiar thing. But if it is peculiar, it is according to the individual; if it is according to the individual, it is an atom. It is concluded, therefore, that nature is an atom; if this is so, then with the removal of one atom, it would be necessary for nature to be removed as well. For of those things that are identical, if the one is removed, it is necessary that the other also be removed simultaneously. Consequently, with the demise of Moses, it would follow that human nature would be removed. But, in fact, with the demise of Moses, nature is not diminished. Therefore, nature is not among the particulars; and if it is not among the particulars, it is not a peculiar thing, because a peculiar thing is of such a nature.
24. Otherwise: If nature is a peculiar thing, it must be predicated only of one; but it is predicated of many, as "rational mortal animal" is predicated of Peter, Paul, Thomas, and every individual man. Therefore, it is not a peculiar thing; and if it is not peculiar, it is common. Or otherwise: If nature is a peculiar thing, and person is likewise a peculiar thing, and nature possesses persons of which it is spoken, it would be found that a peculiar thing is inherent to a peculiar thing, which is not found to be the case. Therefore, it is not a peculiar thing; and if it is not peculiar, it is common. Or otherwise: If that which is predicated of many is a common and universal thing, and nature possesses this, then nature is a common and universal thing. Since, therefore, the supersubstantial, uncreated, and unbegotten substance is a certain divine nature, it is manifest that it itself is also a common and universal thing, because it has been shown that nature is a common and universal thing. If, therefore, the divine and blessed nature is a certain common and universal thing, and divine nature [original-script column: see facsimile] —
[Proceeding from the Latin column as the Greek is cut off]
...not as if it were dictated briefly (to provide these demonstrations thus): God, if indeed He is true God and is believed, either is one thing in nature and person; or one in nature, but many in regard to persons; or vice versa, one in person, but many in nature; or finally, many in both nature and person. But an absurdity emerges from this at once: for that which is in this ratio is polyarchic; and polyarchic is seditious; and, finally, seditious is anarchic and inordinate. But that which is inordinate is the beginning of dissolution; and if this is so, it is also corruptible and about to perish. Now, if perhaps the person is one, but the nature is many, this also is absurd: for that which is of such a kind is composite. For everything that constitutes one thing out of diverse natures is placed in the lot of composite things. Moreover, a composite thing is dissoluble; dissolution is followed by corruption; corruption is accepted by annihilation. It is concluded, therefore, that God is corruptible and falls into nothingness; which is the highest absurdity. If, therefore, being many in both nature and person is proven false; and likewise being one in person but many in nature, it remains that one of the two propositions must be true. If, therefore, our reasoning shows that to be one in nature but many in persons is true and firm; it is clear that the remaining ones are argued to be false, and that this is said truly. That He is one in nature but many in persons is made certain from this. Nature is either a common thing or a property: but if a property, it is individually; which, if individually, is a certain atom: but if it is thus, even with one atom extinguished, it is necessary for nature also to be extinguished at the same time. For if of those things which are the same, one or the other be destroyed, it is necessary that the other also be destroyed together with it. Thus, therefore, with Moses dying, it would have happened that human nature would die. But with Moses dying, nature was not diminished. Therefore, nature is not singular. But if nature is not singular, it is not even a property, because a property is such as to be predicated of one only: but it is predicated of many, as "rational mortal animal," of Peter, of Paul, of Thomas, and of individual men: therefore, it is not a property. And if it is not a property, it is common. Item otherwise: If, therefore, nature is a property; and person is likewise a property; and nature has persons, of which it is pronounced; it is found consequently that a property is something peculiar to a property; which cannot be found in reality. It is not, therefore, a property; and if it is by no means a property, it is common. Furthermore otherwise: If, therefore, that which is predicated of many is a common and universal thing; and nature is so; it follows that nature is something common and universal. Because, therefore, the supersubstantial and uncreated and not-made substance is the divine nature, it is apparent that it itself is also something common and universal; since we have already shown nature to be a common and universal thing.
CONFUTATION FIRST.
Adversary of the sentiments of the first chapter of Mohameda.
26. After our discourse has laid out, by means of a doctrinal method and no less by syllogistic demonstration, the consubstantiality and parity of the Holy Trinity, and the unity and monarchy (as much as can be done in those things that are above reason and are believed),
is a thing, just as nature itself is a thing. But a common thing is not common to one, just as the nature of an angel and of a human is not of one, but of many. Likewise, the universal thing—that which is spoken of over many, as the methods of logic declare—makes it clear, therefore, that the divine nature also belongs to many, if indeed it is a common and universal thing; for this has been agreed upon. 25. If, then, the divine nature belongs to many, as a common and universal thing, and each nature, being common and universal, is common to nothing else but those in which it is contemplated and of which it is predicated—and these would be the proper hypostases—it is concluded, therefore, that the divine nature, being common, is predicated of many hypostases and is contemplated in many, which is true and consonant with the notions of the Scriptures. If, therefore, that which is one in nature is many in its properties, that is, in its hypostases, as our argument has truly shown, it is manifest that that which is one in nature is proven so in hypostasis. But if the divine and blessed nature is one by nature, yet many in the hypostases in which it is contemplated as a common and universal thing, and it transcends all created nature, being characterized more by the "one" than by the "many" (for the difference in God is somehow brief and faint, while there is unity and identity in all things, unchangeably, except for the unbegotten, the begotten, and the proceeding; whereas in creatures it is the reverse: there is much difference and little identity), it is manifest that the many hypostases in which the one and unchangeable nature is contemplated are not an infinite multitude; for an infinite number is not connate with the one, but is alien to it, nor can it simply be extended beyond the number three. Since the multitude itself stands aloof from the one, it is clear that an infinite multitude is entirely alien and abhorrent to it. For if the beginning were a multitude, that which immediately participates in it would necessarily be more of a multitude than that which is more remote. But since unity is the beginning of the divine nature, the number of the divine hypostases that are connected to it must of necessity be the absolute minimum and the most remote from a multitude. Such, therefore, is the Trinity; for neither can duality exist, because it would not be a multitude, nor anything else, because it would not be primal. It is manifest from these things, therefore, that God, being one by nature, is known and rightly believed and glorified by those who think rightly as Christians in three hypostases; and this God for us is the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit.
FIRST REFUTATION AGAINST THE THOUGHTS OF MOHAMMED IN HIS FIRST CHAPTER
26. A Since therefore the discourse has, by scientific method and syllogistic demonstration, well represented—as far as possible regarding things that are above reason and yet exist and are believed—the consubstantiality and identity of the holy Trinity, and the one divinity and monarchy, and has established its own dogma, it is now time to consider that most filthy and pseudonymous book written by the Arab Mohamed with a deceitful pen, and to see whether it contains anything of truth or soundness. We therefore begin our refutation of these things here. For, since the most impious book of Mohamed is open to many refutations—for the whole thing is rotten and, as it were, a crumbling ruin of an edifice built of putrid material, having nothing firm or stable—we propose, above all, the argument that the whole foundation of the edifice seems to be missing; for we who pursue the overthrow of such buildings—namely, those who wish to overturn them when their supports appear to be destroyed—all at once bring the whole building to the ground. C The first mechanism against this rotten structure is its comparison with the divine Scripture. For because Mohamed, having abandoned every kind of doctrine—or rather, having rejected it—constituted as the head of his own speech the claim that he had received an allocation from God, and since we ought in the first place to compare the Scripture which was divinely dictated to us with his book, so that once the light of an accurate comparison has caught hold of his absurdity and incoherence, all his things may be revealed as false, vain, and therefore to be rejected and spat upon, having been shown to be, for the rest, entirely false, vain, and for that reason having been exposed as worthy of blame.
27. C Each book of the divinely inspired Scripture, if looked at separately and by itself, even if it aims at one scope of religion, yet nevertheless contains particular matter, as its title indicates, which openly displays the whole of the argument: just as the inscription of each of the individual volumes invites any profane person, to say nothing of the initiate, to hearing and reading. For example, for the sake of example, Moses and the rest, they have the whole of the argument that corresponds to their title, and that which pertains to their argument, and thus they narrate. Therefore, Genesis, those things which are proper to Genesis; Exodus, those things in Exodus that were accomplished; Deuteronomy, finally, those things which were again sanctified; the later books also, respectively, and, so to speak, have been inscribed in a manner suited to their nature. Similarly in the Prophets, in the Sapientials, in the books of Kings, if you inquire, you will find, with the Holy Spirit as guide in this also, that they display their sincerity and wisdom. For it behooves the teacher of the human mind, concerning the things which produce sound belief, to present them beforehand without error; and after a person has tasted of the intellectual sense of righteousness, then he may become a student of the things further on.
28. But the most deplorable and foolish little book of the Arab Mohamed, beyond the blasphemies with which it teems against the Most High, with all its chaotic and
loathsomeness, neither preserves the series of common and similarly related subjects—for it provides neither the form of prophetic discourse, nor historical, nor legislative, nor theological, nor of anything else issuing from a sound and prudent mind—but possesses everywhere a completely unadorned and utterly perturbed composition; nor does it even offer titles conformable to the subject matter. If, therefore, he is seen to be perfect in his knowledge of all men, how can he be lacking? How will he be able to show as a superior-to-human opinion that which seems to be carried off from heaven, which — as he says — he affirms to have been sent down? Since it is so far removed from human understanding, as far as the nature of the irrational is distinguished from the rational; and not just from that, but from that invincible and superhuman power and wisdom, in which are all the treasures of wisdom and hidden knowledge. For when he aims to write something to God, or to write what he claims to represent as by God, he cannot tell when, or how, whether by vision, or by some other manifestation, or by what witnesses—as it were, for Moses, more than six hundred thousand people bore witness; and indeed it was written on tablets, as on stone—in the law given to Moses. For he says so himself, that he received it unwritten, neither on parchment, nor in the work of human hands, and to be unable to demonstrate what is expected—since it is destitute of all wisdom, divine and human. And what is most grievous, since he—of that blessed and divine nature, as something foreign and extraneous, or rather hostile—brazenly lies; for what is more hostile to the goodness of God than to say that He rejoices in the perdition of men, and sets nothing by their destruction? What sufficient argument can one bring forward, if he does not deny that this is the result of demonic inspiration?
29. Therefore, whatever must be done or said is divided into three parts: namely, the divine, the human, and the demonic. And the Arab, destitute of the former two—that is, the divine and the human—has necessarily fallen into the third. That he possesses neither divine illumination nor human wisdom is admitted, and is frequently made manifest from other things; for human wisdom is either theological, or physiological, or ethical, or historical, or something of this sort. And these are noteworthy. But the author of this trifler’s writing, having enjoyed neither, but having rambled on in his theology in a somewhat stammering fashion—having been carried away into it, calling it, as he does, spherical—that is to say, [original-script column: see facsimile]
that God is a sphere, or rather, as he himself put it, he who surrounds all things. For the shape of a sphere could not be received in any other way; and according to them, if it is a material sphere, it can neither be heard, nor will it be understood, nor will it act, if it happens to be moved by another, and especially if it is borne headlong irrationally. Have mercy on us, O God! When he speaks about God as having the heaven, he is speaking of something that many of the Greeks have refuted, and thus the argument is confirmed as demonic. He knew nothing of natural philosophy, even if he dared to propose it, saying (15) that man is formed from a leech, and that the sun immerses its hot water toward the evening. But as for ethics, what do they say? "Be reconciled to your souls, and be an enemy to all, according to what is hostile to you." And as for history, as much as he misrepresents the Lord, and our Lord Jesus Christ Himself, as having done and said such and such things—and those who read it realize this—for he even says that the Mother of Christ was the sister of Moses, and that Christians lived with Moses in the desert (19). That I may not now enumerate everything without proof, I shall reserve these matters for refutation by demonstration later, by the grace of God. In the meantime, I have recounted, in brief, only as much as was necessary to show that the mind of Mohamed was devoid of divine and human wisdom, and that he was completely unendowed with the gifts commonly given to men by God.
30. This entire little book consists of one hundred and thirteen chapters; in the first, indeed, after that which seems to take the place of a prologue, he exhorts men—those who have been deceived—not to harbor doubt in their minds concerning this fraudulent writing of his, as if it were written falsely.
On the theology of the Arabs: that they are destitute of all wisdom.
But since every discourse and action is divided into three parts—the divine, the human, and the demonic—the Arab, being deprived of the two—namely, the divine and the human—has been carried by necessity into the third. That he is deprived of divine illumination and lacks human wisdom is evident from this and from many other factors. For human wisdom either deals with theology, or natural philosophy, or ethics, or history, or something of that nature; and these things in a worthy manner. But the author of this nonsense, having been gifted with none of these in a noble way, not even in any introductory classification—or even if he babbled—was carried into theology, and he broke out into this godless utterance (15) that God is spherical.
Nay, he says, “[the] solid, the spherical, is the seed of generation;” for he deems Him entirely bodily. For it would not otherwise accept the shape of a sphere. Since the sphere is, according to him, material, it will neither hear nor see—intellectually, that is—nor will it act, if it happens to be moved by another, and especially [by] being swept headlong irrationally. Spare us, O God, those who say his words, even if they are against him! But if he says that the heaven itself is God, as many of the Greeks have babbled, then the statement will be true, in having called him demoniacal.
Regarding physiology, he either knew nothing at all, or if he dared to peer into it, he says that man is generated from a leech, and that the sun is submerged into warm water as it is carried toward evening. As for ethics, what does he say? “Coalesce with your souls, and be hostile to everyone, just as everyone is hostile to you.”
As for history, how many things he falsely alleges about Moses, and about our Lord Jesus Christ Himself, as if He had done and said these or those things, even as you who read [it] perceive! For both he claims the Mother of Christ is the sister of Moses, and that the Christians were with Moses in the wilderness. And so that I may not seem to refute these things without proof by enumerating them all now, I shall save these for the refutation that shall, by the grace of God, be made for each of them later. But for now, as much as was needed only to show that the arrogant mind was devoid of divine and human wisdom, and [lacking] the gifts divinely given to men of which he was deprived, I have enumerated in summary.
30. For the whole of this little book is issued in one hundred and thirteen chapters. In the first discourse, in the writing that seems to hold the place of a prologue, he makes an exhortation to those misled by him not to harbor any doubt in their minds concerning this deceptive writing of his, [thinking] that it was written falsely. And just as to make his deceit credible, when he knows that the former holy Scriptures must be accepted, he commands that this one also—ignorant barbarian that he is—be accepted by his followers, not knowing that he is working toward his own undoing. Moreover, he provides an excuse everywhere against those who do not believe his irrational, blasphemy-filled speech against him who swears by himself—even God, who is good and loves mankind, who does not wish for the death of a sinner, but [wishes] for him to turn and live. The meaning of such words is indeed that God renders this scripture believable to those who understand; he does not hold back those who do not believe from [the act of] believing. Yet when the opposite is the case—which is to say, those who are foolish and believe this [scripture] do not wish to be deceived, and those who do not believe, he himself helps so that they may not be deceived. And that these sayings may become clearer, it results from this: that those whom God wishes to save, He prepares for obedience to His command; but those whom He does not wish to save, He denies the intelligence and ability to listen, and the ability to carry out what is pleasing to Him; and thus He condemns [them] to punishment. And from this comes the consideration of enormous blasphemies: that God prevents those who were intended for good works; that He acts against His own laws by ordaining [them]; and that although He does not wish for all to be saved, He yet proffers saving precepts to all, not as an aid to receiving a reward, but as a pretext for reproach. Thus God behaves as if He refused to reward everyone, or as if He were mocking His creatures, having no one for this truth to whom He shows reverence, either because He rejoices altogether in the destruction of men, or because He retains a vengeful will, because of the enmity felt toward our progenitor, or because He is naturally the cause of evils and cannot help but do evil.
34. Such and so great are the sentiments the most ignorant barbarian utters, putting them forth from the beginning to resolve the doubt, as if he thought that those who wish to be entangled by his other deceits would be satisfied. And it should be noted in all that rustic libello, that the ignorant author knows that it is quite unbelievable to Christians that he has fabricated this myth and barbaric religion, and that he purloins the divine promises contained in the divine Scripture, and that he does not even promise all these things, but only paradise in exchange for inheritance to those who obey him—and this most miserably to women.
And, in order that the deceit might be shown as plausible, he commands that it be accepted equally with the first holy Scriptures, and that this one be added to them, the barbarian not knowing that this is being done for his own overthrow. Continually, and always against those who disbelieve the unsuitability of his words, he sets forth an apology that is full of blasphemy against the good and merciful God who has sworn by Himself that He does not want the death of the sinner, but that he should repent and live; it is this: that God makes those who have trusted in such a writing understand so as to believe, but those who disbelieve, He Himself prevents from believing; yet the matter is truly the reverse, because God does not want those who trust in this vanity to be deceived. And so that what is said may become clearer, the argument relies on this: that God, wishing to save those whom He saves, adjusts them to be obedient to every command of His; those, however, whom He does not wish to save, He does not give to them understanding or the ability to listen and do what is pleasing to Him, and according to this He hands them over to punishment. From this, it is possible to perceive the multitude of absurd blasphemies: that God is a hinderer of those destined for good; that He legislates things contrary to Himself, not wishing to save all, yet proposing the saving commands to all, as an occasion for blame and not as a contribution to rewards; that He does this entirely out of envy, or because He is unable to reward all, or as if mocking His own works, having no one whom He will revere on account of such truth; or as if He simply takes pleasure in the destruction of men; or as if He remains resentful because of the indignation felt toward our forefather; or because He is by nature the cause of evils and cannot but do evil. B 31. These and such things the most ignorant of barbarians pontificates, thinking right from the opening to resolve the difficulty regarding why not all have wished to be subjected to his deceit. And this must be noted throughout that rustic little book: that having known, the one who trifled this together, that his fabricated mythology and barbaric religion were altogether unacceptable to Christians and Jews, he has purloined the divine promises lying in the divine Scripture—and not even all of these, but he lies about the paradise alone, bequeathing it in promise to those who obey him; and this he does most wretchedly with [the promise of] women, following the portion of the writing that is thought to hold the place of an introduction.
With fair and beautiful women they will have intercourse; and what is even more horrible, before the very sight of God—for God, he says, is not ashamed. He fabricates their resurrection as being so entirely fleshly and material, and in no way different from the present life, that they partake of food and drink and the evacuations of the body. To those, however, who do not obey him, he brandishes the punishment threatened in the Gospels against those who commit insults against God, so that by using what is familiar to those who remember them as objects of desire or aversion, he may be easily accepted.
And it is necessary not to be ignorant of this artifice: that he does not refuse to mention the names of the holy prophets, so that by the appellation of these venerable men, the deceit of his godless Agarenism may be unsuspected. For he attempts to refute the truth, which is proclaimed only in name, by openly showing it to be a fraud by his actions; [he acts] as if the Law were not sufficient to save those who are tutored by it, unless they were to be persuaded by him also.
Furthermore, he makes various mentions of our Lord Jesus Christ Himself, and declares Him to be one of the remaining prophets, having only this as an exception: that as the Word of God, He was born of a Virgin through the Holy Spirit, and on account of His impassibility toward Him, was assumed into the heavens—though he who says this does not know what he is saying. In this way, it is clear that he is employing deceit, since indeed he openly slanders Him. For he says that He was asked by the Father if He were God, and that He denied having proclaimed Himself to be God; for he thinks it likely that this was unknown to His Father.
32. In addition to these things, it is also safe to know that everything which he brings forward from the Old Testament and from the New is falsified; and not only this, but also whatever is not even written at all, nor is in agreement with Divine Scripture—his perversity being clearly stripped bare and triumphed over by this fact. There are also collected many monstrosities of his Manichaean abomination: names of certain demons, and oaths through them concerning blasphemous things; invocations to the dwellings of demons; and confessions that apostles and prophets were very often chosen by the God who is proclaimed in such a scripture of his. And a letter [of the alphabet] written in a sorcerous manner, and sentences altered by him, are very often found with him. When [the question was put to him], God answered him: "I know that you spoke this word."
And he teaches by symbols that it is not, he says, either old or new, but something in between, [having] a saffron color, wild and untamed; and he barely persuades them to believe this—these are the triflings of his own inventions, which were put forth so that the incompatibility of his agreement with the Divine Scripture might be shown; through which things he hopes to be believed to be the truth.
34. But that atheist, truly manifesting his own impudence and raving against the glory of God the Father, and trying to draw this to himself in a diabolical madness—just as he was once accustomed to speak with Moses, and as he also says that he sent our Lord Jesus Christ—he says: "We brought [proof] to Moses, and we sent Apostles with him; and we brought to Jesus, the Son of Mary, the revelation; and we strengthened him through the Holy Spirit." When writing these things, what does he think he is proving? Indeed, he says that this barbaric writing is to be accepted as if it were divine: not knowing, however, that he is very far from the mark that he hoped to attain. For who, if he were to speak with him, and having nothing to rebuke in Moses, would say, "Did you send Jesus?" For if it is right, and it was truly done, he will reply: "There was nothing to rebuke in Moses, but Jesus was sent for the perfecting of his instruction; and not only was he sent to change the burdensome carnal custom to a more suitable manner of the Spirit, but also, in turn, to this very one [I would respond]: 'But you, oh [person] who has known Jesus, why have you issued this newer and third writing? Is it because it is the same and equal, or greater, or does it even teach lesser things?' But greater things are impossible for human nature. For what is greater than to hate the world and the body for the sake of God's commandment, and furthermore to be able to hate one's own soul clearly? Yet it is established that nothing of this sort is contained in this barbaric book; indeed, nothing at all that is lofty or good is contained therein; but, on the contrary, it is entirely full of beastly stupidity and atheism. Again, if he is said to teach [them], what is the use of the Law and the Gospel if there is a need for the Law and the Gospel to rise up? For if he had the former, he would be saying the Law, [and that too] perfectly; and as for the Gospel, I mean that which makes [them] firm and serves both [if one] has neither—for the sake of salvation, let him take heart, [even if] some cause imperfect things, and others [reject] the lofty as being above human nature. But nothing of the sort is seen in his teaching: it remains, therefore, that Mohamet's writing, or [that which teaches] lesser things, was given. But if this had been the cause, it is very ridiculous: for sublime things—namely, the middle ones—having been abandoned, to follow a life that crawls on the ground and is beastly, which looks to nothing lofty. Besides, that which is in the highest degree naturally repugnant! And those things which are in a better lot are opposed by worse [things]: which is truly the case of this book, for it is in every way exactly contrary to the Gospel of Christ. If, therefore, the Gospel is the perfect Law; and perfection confirms that which is more; barbaric writing attempts to overturn [it].
It attempts to overturn both that which perfects and that which is perfected; it is not, therefore, the Scripture of God, but the scripture of the barbarian who fights against God. It remains, therefore, that it is the work of the Antichrist.
35. Then he makes mention of Solomon, and contends that he was not deceived by any error, even though divine Scripture openly affirms that he gave himself over in his old age to the worship of idols; a sentiment which is completely contrary to holy Scripture. He also mentions two angels, whom he says came to Babylon, whose names he says are Arioth and Maroth, and says that they were teachers of evil to those who wanted it. Furthermore, he says that the marriages of the androgynous are dissolved by them, and that they [the demons] do these things; but he says that they received this as a concession from God. But divine Scripture does not know of these angels; unless, perhaps, this barbarian claims they are demons known only to Satan and by him alone. One must also observe this in the vanity of this barbarian’s words, that he mentions demons by name and proclaims them; yet in this place he pretends to call them abominable, and likewise their leader, the devil; so that he may entice the simple, persuading them that these things are not a figment of a wicked spirit, but come from the One who reproves demons. Paul, having made more progress in these trifles, thinking himself now to be above everyone, immune to the opposite opinion, and made a teacher for others to mend, slanders the Jews and Christians as being at odds with one another. C He claims that some are pressing forward towards the glory of the Father, while others are trying to surpass him with another opinion, though he says both have fallen from their hope due to the error inherent in each dogma; but he decides for himself that he alone is the heir of paradise, without being asked. He is like someone who, when others are litigating among themselves over a talent and citing their own justifications, injects himself into the middle, declaring that none of you are right, because you are at odds with each other; for I claim that I have done nothing, nor have I fought with anyone. And the one who reproved others for contending one against another, is not ashamed to do the same against them.
36. Furthermore, what demonstration does he have that he received this book from heaven, when he cannot even say, as he does well in truth, that if he were not sent from God he would be subjected to endless reproofs and vituperations? He hopes to dispel the suspicion of a lie; he swears by the sun, the moon, and the stars, and by the lampyris, by the beasts, by the runner-dogs, by trees, and by other unknown barbaric names (which I believe are in fact the demons, and thus he swears by them).
And he seems therefore to destroy the Law as well; and to destroy the things that are perfect and that are being perfected is not the office of God. Therefore the writing is not from God, this book of the barbarian which opposes God; it remains, then, for it to be the work of the Antichrist, that is, of the adversary of Christ.
35. Afterwards, he mentions Solomon and maintains that he was not led into deception, although the divine Scripture openly shows that in his old age he gave himself to the worship of idols; in saying these things, he speaks everywhere in opposition to the Scripture. He also mentions two angels whom he says came down to Babylon, whose names are Haroth and Maroth, whom he calls teachers of evil to those who desire it. Furthermore, he says that these angels broke up the marriages of those who were joined together, and he speaks of them as doing evil, yet he feigns that they themselves also received this permission from God. Now, the tradition of the divine Scripture does not know these angels, unless perhaps one might say that they are the demons of his father, Satan, known only to him alone.
