For he who departs from the one falls into the other; and the word of the Lord is untruth-proof, that "he who does not honor the Son does not honor the Father." It is necessary, therefore, for those who believe in Christ to be forbidden from saying that the Agarenes worship the true God. If they say this, they will be forced to say that Abraham and Moses did not worship the true God, and that the Lord is lying—which is impossible for the Truth-itself to do; and that the Scripture of the ancients does not signify in various ways the persons of the Father and the Son, such as: "From the womb, before the morning star, I have begotten you." And: "Your throne, O God, is forever and ever; a rod of justice is the rod of your kingdom. You have loved justice and hated iniquity; therefore God, your God, has anointed you forever," and the like. But if the faithful confess that the truth is not a lie, and that the followers of Abraham were God-fearing, they will necessarily pronounce the Agarenes to be impious and atheists, and not by any means God-fearing.
XXX. Against the Agarenes who blaspheme the divinity of Christ, and who foolishly say, "He is not God," and on that account think that the region of the Christians has been given to them as a spoil.
ρδ'. Although it was to be expected that they would be rebuked from the very beginning, that is to be overlooked, because there is One who will rebuke them as they deserve; yet, having preemptively adopted common notions for the solution of such an impasse, we shall refute them from those things admitted by them, as they think wrongly in all things and in this. The divine, as we teach them, they confess to be good, benevolent
toward men, wise, just, and powerful, even if they worship another under his name. As he is therefore good, he wishes to benefit our souls, if possible, and our bodies, stirring the soul from sensible things to intellectual ones; and as he is wise and just and powerful, he always prefers the soul to the body. For of men, some are religious, others not. And of the religious, some are good, others wicked. Of these, some are curable, others not. Curable ones are cured partly through prosperity, partly through calamity. Again, some heal themselves, others are amended by others. And of the good, some are saved in tranquility, others through labors obtain grace. But of these, some suffer on their own account, others on account of others. Some suffer both on account of others and on account of themselves, either as being about to become more approved, or as still lying in defect. Of the wicked, some fare prosperously, others calamitously: perhaps for those who are not evil in mind, future punishment is subtracted, on account of present pain; or they are punished on both sides on account of their consummated malice. But the wicked live prosperously, with every delay taken away from them. Therefore, pain and joy, being opposites, yet happening to the same human race, have caused error in many, as if those dear to God are always in good spirits, while those hated by God suffer. But it is not to be thought so; rather, it is to be known that the genus of joy is twofold: one is sensible and temporal, the other intellectual and eternal. And the account of pain is the same. Thus, the just also rejoice, as Abraham; the unjust also rejoice, as those who were under Pharaoh. Conversely, the just suffer, as Jacob fleeing from Esau and serving Laban; also the unjust suffer, as Amalek and the Egyptians. Behold therefore, in one and the same just man, there is pain and sadness; and in one and the same state of an unjust man, there are pain and sadness. Someone will perhaps say: Why these things, and whence the inequality in equals? For cold and heat are not seen together in fire. I concede, indeed; for neither is the just [person] equal at once and unequal: but in those capable of justice and injustice, pain and joy are found.
105. Since therefore it has been said that pain is either temporal and tolerable, or eternal and intolerable; and likewise that joy is either temporal and briefly pleasing, or eternal and long-blessing; the Lord, the lover of our souls, whose benefits are hidden and perpetual, alternately according to his wisdom granting each of these — for temporal joy giving eternal pain, and for temporal pain giving eternal joy — he did not hesitate to lead Adam, who had been deceived by pleasure, from joy into joy, and to thrust Cain from pain into pain. This is what creates perplexity for the barbarians, and on that account drives them to speak foolishly. But come, the fact that Christians are religious is by no means to be learned from the barbarians: for "the sons of men are liars; and every man is a liar," but from the innate truth which is Christ, and from the Spirit of truth who proceeds from the Father, and who spoke under the Law and the prophets; and finally from the grace itself which perfects us. That
the Agarenes are impious has already been demonstrated, is currently being demonstrated, and will surely be demonstrated. However, that among us there are both the useful and the useless, we ourselves see and confess. But since our affairs are steered by the wisdom and goodness of God (for it is not the case that, because of us, He ceases to be good and wise and just and powerful in word, as we have said, for all divine Scripture teaches this), how then will those ignorant barbarians, having with difficulty grasped a judgment in the matter of distinction, and that too in a most wretched way, be able to limit that divine wisdom so ignorantly, as if God knew nothing more than the Agarenes? If, therefore, Christians are religious—and among them some are useful and others not, yet it is the office of God to provide for the salvation of both—they do not therefore plunder us as if we were atheists, but as those corrected by God for our stubbornness, sustained when we have grown weak, checked when we have been arrogant, and chastised by His incomprehensible wisdom in various and manifold ways. Nor, again, are those people religious just because they enjoy temporal prosperity; for neither were the Egyptians religious, who nonetheless oppressed the good servants of the Lord with the yoke of slavery. And just because someone may truthfully say that migration from place to place is motion, it is not therefore to be believed that there is no other kind of motion; for by mentioning one, he has omitted all other kinds. Nor, because the just have sometimes prevailed over the impious, as Israel over the land of Canaan, will these Agarenes be believed in claiming that they have prevailed over us in the same way. For impious men have also existed who have prevailed over the pious most cruelly; the Psalmist is a witness: "O God, the nations have come into your inheritance, they have defiled your holy temple." It is not, therefore, because Christians hold a perverse view of God that the Agarenes devastate their territory and that it has been handed over to their looting; but rather, as the word of truth affirms, they are being corrected for their sins.
106. We, then, being weak but following the word that loves the truth and refutes falsehood, have inflicted certain blows against the most shameless face of the lying word of Muhammad. But those who possess greater strength and confidence in demonstrating the solidity of the truth, to them, without a doubt, a reason of invincible eloquence will counsel that they should rightly subject the blasphemies of Muhammad to appropriate scourges and tortures, and finally, bind him with indissoluble chains, shut him away in inaccessible prisons, and condemn him to silent muteness; so that the saying might be fulfilled: " The mouth of those who speak wicked things shall be stopped."
The end of the refutation against the impious Muhammad.
BY THE SAME NICETAS OF BYZANTIUM
demonstrative exposition of the Christian dogma, from common notions, and dialectical method, and natural arguments; and deduced by syllogistic artifice. Likewise, a refutation of the letter of the Hagarenes, sent to Emperor Michael, son of Theophilus, for the sake of accusing the faith of the Christians.
1. Truth is a thing difficult to capture, and one that can escape even those who are extremely sharp-sighted; unless, perhaps, the soul, by dispersing the density of this bodily darkness, and persuading the senses to close or rest, manages to reach some trace of the truth itself. Certainly, as long as the intellectual eye is still occupied by the smoke of material things, experiencing a sort of stinging passion from them, it will perhaps not recognize the truth lying even before its feet, let alone that which is very far removed; and the more intensely it feels the attraction of these [material] things, the more dense an ignorance of the truth it will suffer. Since, therefore, the knowledge of truth is twofold—the one considered concerning the creation, the other concerning the Creator—as much as creatures are below the Creator, so much does the pure knowledge of the Creator excel. Hence, those who have attributed the knowledge of all things to reasonings alone have been found to have wandered far from it. But those who, having sent word to every pleasure of life, are exclusively occupied with the investigation of this knowledge, with God offering them a helping hand, both surely grasp it themselves and lead others who follow in their footsteps along the straight path to it. Therefore, by the former way, found among those who wish to discourse upon divine things, the second will undoubtedly follow; and conversely, this one will not follow unless that first one is attained. If, therefore, those who are held by ignorance or sloth follow those who have reached that goal, they will enjoy excellence no less than them. But if, having excluded the first, they will not even endure to hold this second course, they will no longer be able to escape the charge of wickedness. For if they neither take care to practice a life of virtue throughout, through which it would be necessary to arrive at a certain knowledge of God, nor obey those who have obtained it, but rely solely on their own reasonings, how can it be that they, running along a different path, can grasp what proceeds by another? Nevertheless, free will truly suffers no force. Therefore, because you rather desire, most erudite friend, the demonstrations of the proposed questions from natural reasonings—for your letter clearly requests this in its very introduction; although the account of our faith does not allow this in a primary place, because God deems every mind, not only human, but even angelic
to be beyond his essence, for the sake of your favor, we shall first set forth our opinion concerning God as demonstratively as possible, to the extent that it is feasible; and then we shall examine the strength of your objections that attack our religion. I shall begin from this point. Nicetas repeats from here his long demonstration of the Christian religion, which he had written in the preceding work (in our edition col. 373 to 707); namely: "The most wise and lover of goodness," etc., he confirmed the dogma. Omitted, however, in this repetition are two short passages, namely concerning Mohammed (col. 689), and another concerning the Hagarenes (col. 697), and this for the sake of euphemism, as I think, lest the Mohammedan friend to whom Nicetas is writing be offended. We therefore have not judged it necessary to repeat that entire part of the Christian apologetic, because it is already available in our volume.
2. It is time henceforth to examine also the accusations in your writing against our faith, to see if they are set forth correctly. I shall begin from this point. Your writing says as follows: "And God gave to them," that is, to the created human beings, "a mind to know that the creation is separate, and stands far removed from the likeness to the Creator; and that every work is in need of its maker." I also agree with this, that everything produced is alien to the substantial likeness of the one who produces it; for which reason, let this be accepted as having been admitted by us as well. Then follows the sentence joined to this, which says: "Every parent is prior to his own offspring." Regarding some things, when said simply, this is true; regarding others, however, it is false; and because this is alien to God, and has no place in Him, I will neither admit it nor confess it. But I shall refute it, and I shall explain when it occurs. You, however, taking this as if it were conceded, seem to be trying to trip us up, whether as regards the principle of consubstantiality, or as regards the relation of the begetter and the begotten. The mode of this thing I will now state. For while we confess that the Father and the Son both hold one and the same substance; and that the one indeed, as begetter, is called Father; and the other, as being begotten, is called Son; you have borrowed an axiom from human affairs which says that "every parent is prior to his own offspring," and you have wrongly applied this to the divine nature; which happens rather in the generated and created nature, but does not hold in God. For otherwise many things, indeed, to speak the truth, even numberless things, occur among us which are entirely alien to God. But you, taking this as true concerning God, conclude almost thus: If every parent is prior to his own offspring, then the Son who is believed by us will not be coeternal and consubstantial with the Father. But if, on the other hand, the Son is coeternal with the Father; and the statement is true that "every parent is prior to his own offspring"; the relationship is necessarily separated from the other.
3. It is not strange, O best of men, if an absurdity being granted, a myriad follow; for if anyone were to claim that a man is a stone, he would attach to him many absurdities, or rather, endless ones. In almost the same way, you too, having taken this little premise concerning God—that "every parent is prior to his own offspring"—
you have insidiously constructed a fallacy. Having refuted this, therefore—since it cannot be pronounced of God—we shall avoid absurdity on both sides. For the present case is absent from the contrary. If, therefore, the statement "every parent," being wrongly taken from created nature, leads the discourse of the one speaking into absurdity, since it has no place in God, it is evident that we shall avoid the absurdities, and we shall preserve untouched the coeternal and consubstantial, and the relation of fatherhood and sonship.
"Every parent is prior to his own offspring." O, the amazing thing! This is valid in me and in you, who are human beings by faculty, or anything else, but we become such by being in potentiality, such as parents or learned men, or something else. But in those cases where potentiality will have no place, it is evident that the priority of the parent over the offspring is not maintained. If, therefore, potentiality is alien to God (for potentiality is accompanied by passion, potentiality needs something from without, potentiality is of the genus of that which is imperfect; but God is above all these), it is sufficiently clear that priority does not attach to Him either, because priority does not exist without some potentiality. C "Every parent is prior to his own offspring." This is proper to created nature, this is proper to generation through flux. For of those whose existence is diverse, their generation is equally diverse. Therefore, three contraries having been taken—I mean the created and the uncreated, the passible and the impassible, the prior and the simultaneous—if the corporeal is passible, and after this follows the prior and the posterior, it is clear that the incorporeal is impassible and simultaneous. For in this way logical methods teach us.
4. "Every parent is prior to his own offspring." This is said by one who wishes to establish a law for God, but not by one who speaks the truth. Therefore, the term "parent" is either of the number of those things which are per se, or of those which are relative. But it is not of those things which are per se; for it would not have a relation to an offspring; therefore it is of those things which are relative. But those things which are relative are simultaneous by nature; and those which are simultaneous by nature are at once both and are not; therefore, the parent, insofar as he is a parent, has a relation to that toward which he has the reference, and will not have "prior" and "posterior." If, therefore, the parent will not have "prior" and "posterior," but you say that every parent is prior to his own offspring, it is clear that the parent is not so insofar as he is a parent, but according to something else, just as Socrates or Peter. Since, therefore, the name of parent in God the Father is hypostatic, and according to this alone the property of the hypostasis is denoted; and it has been demonstrated further that what is relative does not have "prior" and "posterior"; it is certain, therefore, that God the Father, who is the parent of his own offspring, does not have "prior." But if he has it not, it is certain that he is simultaneous; and if he is simultaneous, he is also coeternal. Therefore, the Son is coeternal with the Father; and thus, in that in which you thought to circumvent, you have been circumvented. "Every parent is prior to his own offspring." This has place in us; and that it is so, is evident from hence: Every power—to what end? It is evident that it is simultaneous
and that to be able to do anything, one will also do it, if nothing stands in the way. If, then, there is in us an innate power to beget children, but we are hindered, it is evident that something is hindering us. But in God nothing resists or hinders. It is evident, therefore, that generation is also co-natural. And if it is co-natural, it is also simultaneous; and if it is simultaneous, it is not "prior" or "posterior." Whence it is evident that "prior" and "posterior" have no place in God. "Every parent is prior to his own offspring." Whence, my good sir, have you taken these principles? For they who posit nothing in common between the Creator and the creatures deceive themselves, in the same way taking divine utterances concerning God and creatures; and the first lemma is excluded by the second, and again the second by the first. For if every work is inferior to its maker on account of the dignity of priority, but the Word, who is from the Begetter, argues the Maker to be prior to his works, then He would be both inferior and not inferior, the same and not the same, which is impossible.
5. Let us look also at what follows. It is thus: "Since, if you suppose one principle for both, namely of the Father and the Son, the order of the relation which exists from the Begetter to the begotten perishes; and the designation of both is forcibly separated from the Giver of birth and the begotten, the pre-existent and the posterior." C You have freed me from difficulty, my friend, by explaining the mystery. For if I had persuaded myself that the proposition you stated, which says, "Every parent is prior to his own offspring," had place in God, perhaps, even if unwillingly, I would have been compelled by the truth to confess this second one also. But because our discourse has shown that that former one is futile and entirely false in regard to God and foreign to Him, certainly reason will not compel me to weigh the implied proposition. For if there be two things, of which one is held in the place of the antecedent and the other in that of the consequent, it is clear that if the antecedent is conceded, the consequent will also be conceded; but it will not be conceded if the antecedent is in no way admitted. This is explained thus. Let there be man and animal: and let man be taken as the antecedent, and animal as the consequent. It is evident that if man is posited, the animal also will necessarily follow; but it will not follow if the former is not posited. Such is the argument dramatized by you. Therefore, if the statement "Every parent is prior to his own offspring" had place in God, as we confess the Father and the Son to be co-eternal, it is evident that the designation would necessarily be forced to be separated from one another. But since the former is not true, neither is the second; and thus what was said by you has appeared foolish.
6. And otherwise: If on account of the co-eternity of the Father and the Son you say that their relations must necessarily be separated, reason will rather compel, on account of the relation
of co-eternity also to be introduced. And this is shown as follows. The name "Begetter," since it is among those things which are relative, has a relation to the Son. But those things which are relative are simultaneous by nature; and those which are simultaneous by nature both are and are not. It is concluded, therefore, that on account of the relation they are simultaneous; which is true. And why, my friend, do you introduce the contrary? For on account of the co-eternity you seem to us to snatch away the relation of fatherhood and sonship, while on account of the relation of the begetter and the begotten, you seem to destroy the co-eternity and consubstantiality. Another way: If the name "Begetter" bestows existence simultaneously, but those things which are taken by you as simultaneous in existence destroy the relation which subsists between the begetter and the begotten, it is concluded then that the name "Begetter" and the "Begotten," being in a relation, are unrelated; which is absurd.
B 7. You add again thus: "Since if you suppose one principle for both, the Father and the Son." I am struck with wonder, my friend, as to what kind of principle you wish to place in God. For the term "principle" is used in many ways: for one is efficient, such as the architect of buildings; another is material, such as the planks of a ship; another is formal, such as the shape of a statue; another is final, such as the ship for the transport of supplies; another is instrumental, such as the builder's axe; another is exemplary, such as the prototype of a work produced; another is temporal, such as things that are made in time, which come to be and are; another is causal only, just as Seth sprang from a certain principle and cause, namely from Adam. Now, if as from a certain principle and cause you say that there is one principle for the Father and the Son, you will no longer preserve the one as "begetter" and the other as "begotten." For of whom would he be the begetter, if you say the Father and the Son arose as from another principle and cause? You certainly will not call it an efficient principle; for if you concede this, there will be a God of God, and again another of this one, and so on to infinity. Nor will you call it a material principle; for God is not composite and material. But neither is it a formal principle; for what formal principle could the Father and the Son have had? Other than the fact that they would not escape composition. Nor, finally, a final one, for God comes from no cause. It remains for you that it is a temporal principle of the Father and the Son. But if this is so, it happens to them to be generated and to be corrupted, and countless other things. For those things which are in time are such. It is evident that it is impossible to find a principle more ancient than God, and how, my friend, do you weave together a paralogism, assuming that it is to be believed by us? Truly, it has been demonstrated to you that this is foolish.
8. But let us see what you write a little further on: "And God governed all things by His wisdom." I agree to this most gladly; for by His enhypostatic wisdom, which our discourse clearly demonstrates
The definition of "enhypostatic"
His works, and that God is one above all, supersubstantial, just as one of our theologians said in harmony with these things: "For the invisible things of Him from the creation of the world are clearly seen, being understood by the things that are made, even His eternal power and Godhead" [Rom. 1, 20], and matters consonant with these things. Therefore, whether you will or no, you agree with our confessions. But proceeding a little further to the unity and supersubstantiality of God, you write as follows: "For if He had a partner in His kingdom, He would have been liable to opposition, or He would have been in need of an assistant to govern His creation." I am astonished that you write such things; for I see no necessity to introduce any partners of the kingdom, or any need of an assistant for the governing of His world, or whatever you add to these. For since we confess that God the Father has His Son, in all things similar to Himself except for the unbegottenness and the generation—and yet we do not say at all that there is a partnership between the Father and the Son—I do not see how you can be trying to deceive us, or rather catching yourself in your own snares. Your sophism is of this kind: If two things exist, and one is more general while the other is partial, such as raining and the earth being moistened—for raining is less than the earth being moistened; for it does not convert; yet if it is, it is clear that the rest will be, such as raining and being moistened—then, if the rest exists, someone will say that the other thing also exists; he makes a paralogism, as it is called, from what does not convert and from what follows.
9. You have done something similar in what you have written; for there being two things, "common" and "communicant," and the one being more extensive, such as "common," C and the other more restricted, such as "communicant"—for he who communicates is called a "communicant" insofar as he has something in common; yet it is not necessary that whoever has something in common with another be called a communicant to him; for a stone is not called a communicant to a stone, because the substance of both is common. Likewise, a horse is not called a communicant to a horse, since they have one common substance. If, therefore, "common" is more extensive and "communicant" is more restricted, yet we say that the Father has His kingdom common and indivisible with His own Son, you, distorting the language, write: "If the kingdom and power is common with the Son, then the Son will entirely be a communicant of the kingdom of His Father." You are mocking yourself rather than us, making a paralogism in that which does not alternate, and in doing so, you appear rather to be ignorant of the things of which the term "communicant" is rightly and properly said, and for that reason you badly transfer it to God; for the term "communicant" does not appear to be applied to anything else than those for whom the work of each one is accomplished separately and distinctly, and by a voluntary agreement, and by a joining together in the same act, [or] that which is enhypostatic, which exists really as common to the individuals which are under it, and is not [merely] according to substance; that is to say, the species which is considered in the mere thought of the mind.
If two or three or more of these come together for the same thing—for instance, if someone is practicing the art of rhetoric by himself, and then some other person approaches him, and they come together harmoniously to the work of rhetoric by prior agreement and consent—each of these might be called a 'partaker' in this. But as for those in whom there exist by nature, inseparably, the work and the will, the desire and the operation, and an indivisible harmony, how would the name of 'partaker' find place for them? It certainly would not. Therefore, when we speak of the Father doing something, we speak of the Son and the Holy Spirit as doing it together inseparably. And the operation of the One is not divided among the Three. For every operation that proceeds from God, and is named according to various concepts, originates from the Father, proceeds through the Son, and is brought to perfection in the Holy Spirit. For this reason, it is not right to apply the term 'partaker' to the Holy Trinity.
10. You proceed to the [term] "partaker," writing thus: "It was about to experience contrariety and sedition." Oh, the foolishness of your words! If indeed the name 'partaker' had a place with God, perhaps both contrariety and sedition would be found. But since this is not granted, neither will the consequence of it be granted. And thus, although unwilling and against your intent, from what you have added, you have made the proposition clear to us as to how it is properly spoken. For if, when the name 'partaker' is applied to something, contrariety and sedition follow, it will be clear that the term 'partaker' will be appropriate for those who conduct affairs by prior convention and consent—whence sedition and contrariety also arise. For it is only to them that sedition and contrariety occur, due to the mutability of their will. But how could contrariety and sedition gain entry to those whose goodness is immutable? And after a few words, you write thus: "Or, at least, he would be in need of a helper for the administration of the world created by him." Rightly, friend, did you speak. For if the name 'partaker' had a place with the Son, he would have, according to you, [a share in] the contrariety and sedition, and the need for a helper. But since that [term] has been shown to be hollow and invalid, it is clear that these [consequences] are so, too. For if by the positing of something, certain things are posited, then by the destruction of that thing, it is clear that the others are also destroyed. D
11. Your letter further says this: "And according to the extension of the ages, and the contrariety between them, the lordship of him would be extinguished along with the creatures, and one would lord it over the other, and the sympathy of the works would be cut off." This accusation, therefore, O friend, of two principles and contrariety does not apply to us; rather, it ought to be directed by you toward others, who indeed introduce two principles and two gods, a good one and an evil one; and they say that one of them created and maintains and controls the sensible world, and the other, the intelligible world; an opinion which we loathe and strongly turn away from, and those who cherish it we subject to capital punishment; while you...
favor and cajole them. For us, one principle is worshipped with religious devotion, because there is one God, even though he is believed to be threefold in his properties, or persons. But if the diversity of persons suggests to you that there is also a different nature—and that there are as many natures as there are persons—this is the mark of an unlearned, rather than an educated, mind. For nature and person are not the same thing; otherwise, there would be nothing homogeneous among existing things, and what is common would be the same as what is peculiar, a thing which has never been found.
12. And in another way: If the difference in persons grants you that a different nature is introduced, it is necessary that what does not differ naturally, also does not differ personally. It follows, therefore, that those things which are one naturally, are also one personally. Thus, Paul and Peter would be the same naturally and not different, and at the same time the same person, which is ridiculous. For your sophism is what is called a sophism of homonymy. Since the term "one" is said in many ways, as the rules of logic teach—either by genus, or by species, or by number, or by analogy—it is clear that the same applies to "many." For in as many ways as the subject is proposed, in just as many ways is it opposed; for this is what we have been taught. Yet, to those who acknowledge the trinity of persons, you attribute also a diversity of natures. But the matter is not so, as the evidence of things clearly shows and as the disciplines teach. And thus, all your accusations against our faith have been shown to be futile, incredible, and false. Furthermore, with all the accusations destroyed, all the rest will follow for you. But we have already run beyond the proper measure, for ears hate a surfeit of speech. Wherefore, let these things suffice for those who are willing to consider them a little, and by a comparative choice of better things, to follow the truth and repel falsehood. And for this reason, I shall make an end of speaking here.
BY THE SAME NICETAS
Refutation and eversion of the second epistle sent by the Hagarenes to the Emperor Michael, son of Theophilus, for the purpose of accusing the Christian faith.
1. I would have wished, O friend, to first subject the discourse regarding our faith—the Christian dogma, I mean—to demonstrative necessities, and to render it as firm and exact as possible by means of natural reasoning; and then, in turn, to refute those absurd arguments written by you for the sake of slandering our religion. For this is what an antithetical discourse absolutely demands. Now, since against your other epistle, the dogma...
of our faith has—as was fitting—been treated at great length with constructive demonstrations and clearly concluded, let us not seem to falter in writing to you now, if we only construct our retorts from the arguments you sent for the overthrow of our blameless faith. Wherefore, if your slanders against our faith and your other contradictions are rightly aimed, let us examine them here. Your letter runs as follows: "God is one, having no sharer in his own power; and because he has no sharer, he has not a son." I am truly amazed that you write such things; for I do not discern what necessity there is to introduce the Son as a sharer—as it were—or an assistant in the kingdom of God the Father, and all the other things of this kind that you frequently write. For when we confess that God the Father has his own Son in all things similar, except for unbegottenness and birth, and when we do not in any way confess the Son to be a sharer of the Father, I do not know how you wish to deceive us, or how you might be caught by your own wings. For your sophism is of this kind: "If there are two things, and one is greater and the other lesser, such as man and animal; for man is less than animal (because they do not reciprocally alternate), but animal is greater; with these things being so, as if where there is man, there is also animal; but it is not the case that where there is animal, there is also man; if, while animal exists, someone were to say that man also exists, he commits a paralogism, because these do not alternate nor follow one another."
In this place Nicetas repeats the whole ninth article of the prior epistle, namely from the words *toidūton ti* [something of this sort] up to *epi tēs hagias Triados tithesthai* [to be set down concerning the holy Trinity]. We, however, omit it, in order not to appear to be doing what has already been done.
2. Again, he says: "God is one, having no sharer in his own power." That God is indeed one over all things, and that he is the one who grants to all existing things both simple being and well-being, and possesses absolutely no sharer or assistant in any of existing things, as you say, has been demonstrated and confessed and believed by us above. But that the Son of God believed by us is also true God, equal to God the Father, and is rightly confessed and spoken of by us as one God together with the Father and the Spirit—not as a sharer of the God and Father, nor as introduced by us as a sharer of the one principle and one God who is contemplated in the Father and Son and Holy Spirit—becomes clear thus: The one who is called by us the Son of God and true God, if he is, as you say, a sharer of God, that is, of him who is called by us God and Father; or of the one principle and one God who is contemplated in the Father and Son and Holy Spirit. But if indeed he is a sharer of the one God who is contemplated in the Father and Son and Holy Spirit; and it has been demonstrated by us in the refutation of your prior epistle that the Son is one God and one principle with the Father and the Holy Spirit, and is rightly called God by us; therefore it happens that the Son...
is a participant in himself, which is absurd; for no existing thing is said to be a participant in itself. Therefore, the Son is not a participant in the one principle and the one God who is contemplated in the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit; nor indeed of God the Father; for only in those cases is it possible to find a son called a participant of his own father, where there is an entering in of a change of mind, and a presence of diversity of will, and a lack of resources calling for help, and an operation separately cut off. But in those where there is one mind, and an will always unceasing, and an identity of nature, and one power, and an unchangeable will is present, how could a participant enter in? Therefore, those who theologically posit the Son and Spirit in theology do not introduce a participant in the one God, even if they are wrongly slandered by you.
3. Item alter: "God is one, not having a participant in his own power; and because he has no participant, he has not even a son." You have relieved me of a task by declaring the secret; for if I saw the word "participant" as connected to and subsisting in the theological generation, perhaps, though unwillingly, led by the truth, with the extinction of this word I would also remove the principal point from the middle: that is, I would not allow the Son to be proclaimed as God. But because our discourse shows this to be altogether futile and false and not at all following in the theological generation, no reasoning compels me to exclude the principal point, that is, the Son, from the designation of God. For in those things which cohere by a necessary bond, it is evident that with them removed, the principal points also perish; yet it is not so in those things which do not cohere with one another, as the disciplines teach. Therefore, this was the force of your syllogism: "If the divinity of the Son is believed, we shall find that one God has someone participating in him; but God is universal and one, having no participant: therefore, the Son is by no means found in theology." And first indeed, as philosophical methods teach us, the consequence is mistaken. For it has been demonstrated that it does not follow in the theological generation that the Son is called a participant of the Father; therefore, that assertion is without demonstration. Soon we shall consider our theology somewhat, so that we may show more evidently to all the weakness and incredibility of the dogma brought forward by you.
4. What is therefore truly said by us concerning theology is said partly in common regarding the three persons, as concerning one substance or deity, and one God being; for instance, I say the name God, and Lord, and philanthropist, and similar things, which are not numbered together because they are said singularly, as has been demonstrated. But they are partly said privately, which are also in the first place and especially proper to the persons; for instance, I say the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit, which, because they are said privately, are also numbered together: for the numbering together is mainly the cause of those things which are said privately, not of those which are said in common, as has been shown elsewhere; and sometimes it is even found in two
thearchic persons, for example, procession from a cause. And those things which are said in common, and are said in common at the same time of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, as being one God who is also thought of as one, are stated as such, and are predicated properly and particularly of the persons. For the Father is truly God; and the Son is God as from God, and light from light; and likewise the Holy Spirit. But those things which are said privately of the persons are indeed said privately, but they are by no means said in common; because the lesser cannot be predicated of the greater, nor be made equal to them. You may find this also stated regarding created nature, as for example, for the sake of clarity: the term "man," and "mortal," and "rational," each of these is spoken of both in common when taking all individual men together, and privately when considering each one. For Peter and Paul and individual men are at once spoken of by the common term "man," and each is also spoken of privately. For Peter is called a man, and so is Paul, and others similarly.
5. But if, even in our theology, some things are said in common of the three persons, and others privately; and some are predicated both in common, and in common at the same time, and privately as to the part: for when the name of God or Lord is spoken of in common at the same time regarding the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, it is clear that the three are spoken of as one God, in whom divinity exists, and among us it is rightly theologized in this way. But when the name of God or Lord is spoken of privately and as to the part regarding the Father, or regarding the Son or the Spirit, then the Father is called God, not however as if the Father were one God privately and in part and of a different nature; and again the Son as another God; and likewise the Holy Spirit. Away with such blasphemous polytheism from us! Since for us the object of worship is one principle, and there is one God, even if in properties, that is, persons, three are believed. Whence, when we theologize the Son of God, we say he is one other than the Father in person, but not one other in essence and divinity, but the one and the same, as was shown in the former epistle.
6. Therefore, the theology we hold, hanging from common notions and the divine prophets, and having acquired certainty and stability, theologizes the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit as one God, the Trinity. But you, not understanding it thus, but thinking and imagining that the Father is called the one God privately and as to the part; and then that the Son is introduced as it were from outside that one God, have concocted a paralogism; wherefore you falsely conclude thus: "If there is a Son in God, he is also a participant; but no one is a participant in God; therefore, there is no Son." By saying this, you fall into many absurdities. And first, you make person and nature the same in God, which has already been refuted. Next, you do not grant that the Son, together with the Father and the Spirit, is also one God, which is otherwise rightly asserted in our theology; and this, too, has already been demonstrated by us; for this reason
and because of what you have asserted, it has appeared foolish. A Let us see also what is added; it runs thus: "He who made the faith of the Saracens, like light; and there is no other faith through which one can approach God," and whatever else you write in praise of your own faith. But to attack another's faith with abuses, simply and without proof, and to exalt one's own greatly, is most easy and simple for anyone who wishes; but to demonstrate that one's own faith is useful, beneficial, and given by God through arguments, is the business of a man who is religious and possessed of reason. Therefore, you also ought to have done this, and not simply boastfully assert that your faith was made and given by God like light; wherefore we shall endeavor to demonstrate with proofs the error and falsehood of your faith, and the truth and beauty of our own.
7. All who possess reason admit that a good and useful faith functions as a light, and exhorts those who hold it to approach God; the precepts of this faith have the casting aside of the worst and most harmful things, and translate the human race from those things which are contrary to nature to those which are according to nature and supernatural. But the faith whose precepts command the flight from virtue and the intimacy of vice, and drag the human race from those things which are according to nature or even supernatural to those which are contrary to nature, this is entirely base and harmful, and no one can approach God through it. Since therefore your faith commands the intimacy of vices and the casting aside of virtues, and drags those who embrace it from things supernatural, or at least natural, to those that are contrary to nature, it is manifest that it is base and harmful, and that no one approaches God through it. And that this is true, understand from this: all prudent people know that what is contrary to nature is a transition from the natural to the worse; but the supernatural is a transition from the natural to the better. Since, therefore, man was in a way created as a medium between the sensible and the intellectual creation, for this reason he participates in both, that is, in the irrational and in the angelic nature. And he participates in the angelic according to reasoning and discretion, but in the irrational according to the irascible and the concupiscible. However, man is preserved and contained within the boundaries of nature when the rational part dominates the irascible and concupiscible. Likewise, he approaches more closely to the angelic nature and is lifted up to supernatural things when the irrational part of the soul is repressed and suppressed. But when the opposite is observed in man and the passions prevail over reason, and reason succumbs (this occurs in people who use a luxurious and savage manner of life), it is evident that man, doing worse things, migrates from natural or even supernatural things to those which are contrary to nature. Wherefore, the more the irascible and concupiscible lack a rein, the more the passions erupt; and the more they erupt, the more they are increased; and the more they increase, the more man makes common cause with the irrational beasts; and
by doing this, he hastens to be brutalized. For this reason, the prophetic word also describes the man who wallows in the passions, having been made like the beasts, saying: "Man, being in honor, did not understand; he was compared to the senseless beasts, and was made like unto them." (Ps. XLVIII, 13).
8. Since, therefore, your faith proposes and sanctions an intemperate life, and rather the increase and dominion of the passions than of reason, and drives men to gape after worse things—this is precisely what we have said, that it drags men away from the natural and supernatural toward those things which are contrary to nature—it is agreed among us that that faith whose statutes are of such a kind is harmful and vicious, and that no one through it approaches God. Hence, the conclusion is reached that your faith, O Saracens, should be called darkness rather than light, and that no one through it approaches God. But if this is so, it was not given by God either; and you boast and pride yourselves in vain about a faith of false name, or, to speak more truly, an infidelity, and you speak against us. But since our faith prescribes the contrary to yours—for yours prescribes luxury, ours modesty; yours gluttony, ours temperance; yours lechery, ours purity of character—it has been demonstrated that yours is depraved and harmful, neither reconciling one to God, inasmuch as it causes one to communicate rather with the brute beasts; while it is established that our own is good and useful, and renders us friends of God, because it raises men to the condition of angels. And therefore we can confidently say that there is no faith other than our own, we who are Christians, by whose help anyone can draw near to God and participate in the true good. And let this suffice for what has been said so far regarding your faith and ours.
9. But advancing a little further, you strive to show that man was by no means created in the image and likeness of God, as we, trusting in the history of Moses and the realities themselves, have written to you. Wherefore, when you seem to write with a specious pretext as follows: "God is ineffable, indefinable, and incomprehensible. For God always was, and there is none like unto Him. For His height is infinite, and He made His handiwork without any likeness to Himself," you seem from this to conclude that man has not been created in the image and likeness of God. For since God is incomprehensible and invisible and so forth, and no thing that exists is comparable to Him, it is evident that not even man is made in the image and likeness of God, since he also is one of the things that exists; and if this is so, he has not been formed in God's image and likeness. These are the words of a man prescribing laws for God and His creations, yet saying nothing that is true. Surely, if we, while we affirm that man has been made in the image and likeness of God, were to speak of a likeness of substance, which would be undifferentiated, or rather the same as God, and were immediately to confess God to be ineffable,
furthermore, in confessing God to be ineffable, illimitable, incomprehensible, and infinite—and that nothing among existing things is such—we would perhaps be contradicting ourselves, and what you write would seem probable. But since, when we say that man was made in the image and likeness of God, we do not mean a similarity of essence, but rather an analogy that he has toward God, which is also found in creatures, there is nothing absurd in our writing that man was molded in the image and likeness of God. For the history of the genesis of the world according to Moses also persuades us to acknowledge this; therefore, we say "in the image," according to [the faculty of] initiation and free will. Furthermore, there is in man mind, word, and spirit; and the mind is the progenitor of the word within us; and the word is the offspring of the mind; and the spirit is issued through the word. And just as there is no irrational mind without word, so too no irrational word is found without mind, but they coexist at once, and neither precedes the other; and in like manner the word is not without spirit, nor is the spirit, which is expressed into utterance, without word.
10. Since, therefore, the supersubstantial and uncreated substance, which is God, is contemplated in three hypostases—that is, in the Father, and in the Son, and in the Holy Spirit—as has been demonstrated to us and is rightly believed, our mind, being the progenitor of the word within us, has an analogy to God the Father, who eternally and timelessly begat His own Son; and the word within us, being an offspring of the mind within us, has an analogy to the Son who was begotten from God the Father without flux and without passion, and is the true God; and the Spirit within us [has an analogy] to the Holy Spirit, who proceeds from the Father and is poured out and distributed to existing things through the Son. And according to this, man is again said to have been molded in the image of God. And [we say] that man was made in the likeness of God again [through] the perfection of virtues and the participation of goods; because all things that the creative cause—which is God—possesses primarily and causelessly, these man possesses secondarily, participatively, and causally. For instance, He is the first and supersubstantial, life, light, wise, powerful, good, mild, just; and this man also is wise, and life, and powerful, and good, and mild, and just; but God is this by nature and essentially, while man is this by adoption, by grace, and non-essentially. If this is so, we rightly say that man has been made in the likeness of God, bearing as it were the final echo of a reflection. And thus again your writing has been shown to be foolish.
11. Proceeding to another of the things written by us, and attempting to refute this too, you write again: “We behold in the species of wild beasts that the stronger rises up against the weaker, and the
The stronger against the weaker, and the better against the worse, and often it makes its own food of it; there are times when it also destroys it, competing to drive it from its lair, and so forth. In this you seem to have been ignorant of how arguments ought to be brought forward, and how they ought not. For if what we wrote concerning every irrational animal—as regards both those that are of the same species and those of a different one—promised that they all congregate and unite by reason of mutual gentleness, as if sparing their own salvation; and then, finding this not to be observed in some animals, but rather the contrary—that the stronger rises up against the weaker, and the better against the worse, as you indicated in your writing, for example the lion, I say, or the bear and the like—you would indeed have been able to refute our argument stoutly. But because what we wrote does not declare this concerning every irrational animal, you are babbling foolishly, introducing a poorly constructed drama and playing a part. For who, even with the slightest discernment, is unaware that not all kinds of animals are naturally constituted to congregate and follow a social life? For of animals, some are solitary, others gregarious; and of the gregarious ones, some are ordered under a leader, others not. And of both of these, some are civil, and others not, as their physiologies and histories teach. If, therefore, some are solitary and others not, it is evident that it is their nature for some to congregate with those of the same species, and others not to. If this is so, no one possessing a sound mind would ever think or write that "all animals are accustomed to congregate with one another and live together." Nor have we said this. But since there is in the animal that is properly social a most particular sign and characteristic—namely, reason, prudence, and judgment with reason—for this very reason man needs external speech, so that the thoughts lying in the depths may be brought forth into the light and shared with his neighbors. Hence man is by nature a tame animal, and is here properly a social animal. But in all irrational animals, sociability and natural gentleness are not primarily present; yet in some, a desire to converse, live together, and communicate is discerned. But this is established much more among humans. For if what seems to be less exists, how much more exists that which is more; that is to say, how much more convenient and useful is it for a man—being an animal that is pre-eminently social and by his own nature tame—to converse and live together with one of his own kind? For it is with this in view that we have written to you.
12. And again, passing on to another of the points written by us, and attempting in the same way to refute it, you write as follows: "Of killings, some are lawful, others unlawful; and it is unlawful, as it is actually forbidden by God, to kill a believer by a private decision; but it is lawful and permitted to kill him who suggests that a companion is to be given to God, and imagines this one to be endowed with equal power." While you write these things, un-
learned and entirely uninitiated in these matters, you reveal yourself; for you have not understood that every killing, in so far as it is killing, is to be shunned and abhorred, inasmuch as it is an evil; and that all evil is not permitted. And that killing is an evil, is clear from this: Every killing, in so far as it is killing, is a destruction of a man, or is the cause of the destruction of a man. But there is nothing more beautiful or better upon the earth than man, since he is the foremost creature of God among those on the earth. Therefore, it is concluded that every killing, in so far as it is killing, is a destruction of some good or a cause of destruction; and if this is so, it is evil in itself. For the destructions of good things, and the causes of them, are entirely evil; just as the destructions of evil things and their causes are good; for thus our common notions and philosophical disciplines teach us. B If, therefore, all killing, in so far as it is killing, is demonstrated to be evil, it is clear that it is also not permitted; for every evil is not permitted. Yet, sometimes it becomes good by accident. For, of existing things, some are good in themselves, and become evil by accident, such as almsgiving; others are evil in themselves, but become good by accident, such as a lie (7); others are of a neutral nature in themselves, but by accident incline to one side or the other, such as wealth, strength, and the like. And those things which are good in themselves ought always to be done, or at least for the most part; for such is the nature of goods in themselves. The opposite logic applies to things that are evil in themselves, which either ought never to be done, or rarely, and in minimal quantity. For such is their nature. C Those which are of a neutral nature in themselves, it is similar whether they are done or not. That killing is in the number of things evil in themselves, we have already shown. But that which is evil in and of itself and principally, that either ought not to be done at all, or rarely; and this is primarily good: but that which is done rarely by accident [as a] good, that is good in the second place and to a lesser degree. Now, that which is good in a primary sense and in a greater degree is certainly more desirable and better than that which is good in the second place and in a lesser degree, just as the rules of logic instruct us. Therefore, not to kill is better and more desirable than to kill, even if the latter sometimes becomes good by accident. If this is the case, those who strive to abolish killing among men appear better and worthy of greater praise than those who have not determined to act in this way; for the former have embraced the better good. It is therefore proven that we are better, who strive to exterminate killing from among men.
13. But that the killing (8) which is perpetrated by you against us every day is neither of those [things] happening as a good by accident,
(7) For instance when someone lies, so that it may profit someone. Nevertheless, Augustine also censures this, enarr. in Ps. V, n. 7: "There are two kinds of lies, in which there is no great fault, but yet they are not without fault, when we either joke, or we lie so that we might benefit [someone]. The first is such, etc." The second is therefore milder because it retains some benevolence. See the rest there; then many other things...
(8) He speaks of the superstitious and fanatical savagery of the Mahomedans against the Christians, which, beyond the suras cited earlier, we see also in the third epistle of Nicholas, the Greek patriarch, among those published by us in Spicilegium Rom., T. X.
from this it becomes clear: What comes to be as a good per accidens, comes to be by an economy; what comes to be by an economy, comes to be for some good; therefore, what comes to be per accidens, comes to be for some good. But the slaughter of our people by you does not come to be for any other reason, as you yourselves say, except for the sake of your being Saracens; but this is not good: it follows, therefore, that the slaughter of our people by you is not even a good per accidens. And that the sect of the Saracens is by no means good is shown thus: Saracenism is nothing other than the enticement of people away from things natural or even supernatural, toward things contrary to nature; for this we have already demonstrated. Now, that to do this is evil, everyone sees. If, therefore, your slaughters of our people are good neither per accidens nor in themselves; and if that which is good neither in itself nor per accidens ought not to be done at all, then your daily slaughters of our people ought not to be done. For what ought not to be done, is not even lawful. “Some slaughters are lawful, you say, others are unlawful.” I also agree that some slaughters are lawful, namely per accidens, if indeed they are of the number of those goods which turn out thus per accidens. But they are unlawful in themselves; since slaughter, insofar as it is slaughter, has been shown to be of the number of evils per se; therefore let this be taken as something clearly confessed by us as well.
14. Come then, briefly you append what follows, saying: “It is lawful and innocent to slay him who thinks a partner should be given to God, and one gifted with equal power.” I say, how proudly and arrogantly is this spoken by you—or, to speak more properly, with what great stupidity and madness it teems! For, owing to your great stupidity and faithlessness, judging I know not how without reasoning, and not as we preach, but fighting in vain and foolishly blathering, you mock us without cause, as if we introduced certain participants and beings equipollent to the one God and unique principle. For who among us, whether small or great, ever thought or imagined that anyone was a partner to the one God and unique principle or equipollent to Him? Under what pretext do your slaughters of our people turn out to be “just,” as you say? For even if the Son of God is spoken of and glorified by us as true God, and as being Himself homogeneous with God and the Father, nevertheless, it is not defined in our theology that He is a partner to the one principle, or a God existing outside the Father and added to Him as you think (for this also has been demonstrated by us); but He is one God with the Father and the Holy Spirit, and is rightly believed in and glorified by us Christians. If this is so, you rashly cast at us the reproach of the term “partner”; and furthermore, you consider the slaughter of our people by you to be divinely inspired and lawful, as you write; for if, because you say we invent a partner and an equipollent of God, it is lawful to be slain by you,
it is manifest that it is also unlawful to put us to death, after we have refuted this false imputation; and it stands to reason that the slaughter with which you rage against us is unjust. But if the distinction of the persons of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit perturbs and terrifies you, as if it thrust from without, into the one God and one principle, a Son as a partaker and different from the nature of God the Father, this is the mark of an unlearned man rather than a learned one. For nature and person are not the same; otherwise, no existing things would be homogeneous, and the same thing would be both common and particular; which does not happen.
15. Item; if the distinction of persons were to introduce a distinction of nature, it would be necessary that what is not distinct in nature should not be distinct in person either. It follows, therefore, that whatever is the same in nature would also be the same in persons. Socrates, then, would also be Peter, and if by nature they are the same and indistinguishable, they would also be the same in person; which is ridiculous. For this sophism is so called from homonymy. For since the term "one" is said in many ways, as the rules of logic teach us—namely, by genus, or by species, or by number—it is certain that the term "many" also has the same rationale; for by as many ways as the proposition is stated, in just as many ways is the opposite; for that is what we have been taught. Since, therefore, we give the number three to the persons, you introduce the distinction of natures as well. And these matters stand thus, and are evident both from the reality of the facts and as the disciplines teach. And in this way all your calumnies and contradictions against our faith have been demonstrated to be futile, incredible, and false. Now that the calumnies have been refuted, all the rest follows of its own accord. But what has been said so far is more than sufficient, and the superfluity of words is tiresome to the ears. Therefore, these things suffice for those who willing to consider them even a little, and, by the addition or comparison of better arguments, to attain the truth and dispel the falsehood. Here, therefore, I will make an end of speaking.
Theognostus the Monk; Notitia
The man who was cast out by the Byzantines, and by the philosopher and golden-tongued lord Nicetas, the Arab Muhammad, was an orphan, a foreigner, a beggar wandering in every country and city. But coming into Arabia, and making his stay there, he approached a rich widow and hired himself out as a servant to serve her and tend her camels. Being wicked and ironic in his speech, he deceived her and married her, being himself a master. The woman was distressed when she learned this, and was grieved, saying that she had perished by taking a beggar and a demoniac; and she was about to drive him away from her. This wicked man, perceiving this, attached himself to a friend of his who was a monk, who had been exiled from the Great City, and gave him these instructions, saying: "I know that you are known to my wife, and that she wishes to tell you about my affairs, and how I fall down in a daze. You, to this, support me by saying that this was nothing else but a vision of an angel of God, named Gabriel, and that I am a prophet of God." At these things the woman rejoiced greatly and was persuaded; and from that time on until today, what was heard from her has been held fast in Arabia.
